Localization of eigenvectors of non-Hermitian banded noisy Toeplitz matrices
Anirban Basak, Martin Vogel, Ofer Zeitouni

Abstract
. We prove localization with high probability on sets of size of order N / log ⁡ N N/\log N for the eigenvectors of non-Hermitian finitely banded N × N N\times N Toeplitz matrices P N P_{N} subject to small random perturbations, in a very general setting. As perturbation we consider N × N N\times N random matrices with independent entries of zero mean, finite moments, and which satisfy an appropriate anti-concentration bound. We show via a Grushin problem that an eigenvector for a given eigenvalue z z is well approximated by a random linear combination of the singular vectors of P N − z P_{N}-z corresponding to its small singular values. We prove precise probabilistic bounds on the local distribution of the eigenvalues of the perturbed matrix and provide a detailed analysis of the singular vectors to conclude the localization result.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
47A10, 47B80, 47H40, 47A55, 60B20

1.
Introduction and statement of results

1.1.
The setting

The spectrum of non-Hermitian operators is inherently sensitive to tiny
perturbations due to the fact that their resolvent may be large even far
away from the spectrum. This is in stark contrast to the Hermitian case, where
due to the spectral theorem, the norm of the resolvent is effectively controlled by the
distance of the spectral parameter to the spectrum. This spectral instability
of non-Hermitian operators, although traditionally an adversary for numerical
analysis
[
70
,
72
]
, has recently shown itself at the origin of beautiful new results
in a variety of contexts. For instance in the theory of non-linear partial differential equations
for instance, spectral instability may help to explain the blow up in finite time of
solutions to certain non-linear diffusion equations which, when solely studying
the spectrum of the linearized operator, were expected to have stable solutions

[
58
,
54
,
30
]
.

In mathematical physics non-Hermitian operators appear in a large variety
of subjects, such as open quantum systems
[
50
,
51
,
52
]
.
In quantum mechanics, the study of scattering systems

[
26
,
37
,
60
,
66
,
48
,
25
]
naturally leads to the concept
of
quantum resonances
which can be described by the eigenvalues of a
non-Hermitian operator obtained from
a complex deformation
[
66
]
of a
Hermitian quantum Hamiltonian. In physical models an “ideal” operator can
be perturbed by many different sources, some of which are uncontrolled by
experimentalists. To account for these error terms disorder is introduced and,
in view of the phenomenon of spectral instability,
it is therefore relevant to investigate the influence of
random perturbations
on the spectral data of non-Hermitian operators.
The recent works
[
62
,
41
,
24
]

investigate for instance the distribution of the quantum resonances of
random Schrödinger operators such as the
celebrated Anderson model
[
6
]
.

In this paper we consider large deterministic non-Hermitian
N\times N
Toeplitz
matrices
P_{N}
with small additive random perturbations. It was
shown in a series of recent results that the spectra of such matrices, apart from
finitely many fluctuating outliers
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
65
,
64
]
, mimic the
absolutely continuous spectra of
the associated infinite dimensional Laurent operator on
\mathds{Z}
. This is particularly
striking since a perturbation of size
O(N^{-\infty})
is sufficient to produce this effect,
whereas the spectrum of the unperturbed matrix is far from the spectrum of the Laurent
operator.

The aim of this paper is to discuss the eigenvectors associated with the eigenvalues
of such perturbed Toeplitz matrices. Are the eigenvectors
localized
or

delocalized
? The precise meanings of these notions vary over
different subjects, however, they all serve to capture how much an
\ell^{2}
normalized
eigenvectors
concentrates on
or
spreads out over
certain parts of its support.

In random matrix theory there are several ways of testing for localization or delocalization
of
\ell^{2}
normalized eigenvectors
\psi
. One way is by comparing their
\ell^{p}
norms
for
2<p\leqslant+\infty
with
N^{1/p-1/2}
. Complete delocalization is said to occur when

\|\psi\|_{p}\,=O(N^{1/p-1/2})
(up to some logarithmic factors) since
N^{1/p-1/2}

is the
\ell^{p}
norm of the fully delocalized vector

(N^{-1/2},\dots,N^{-1/2})
. Conversely localized eigenvectors have a large
\ell^{p}
norm,
as for instance the fully localized vector
(0,0,1,0,\dots,0)
has
\ell^{p}
norm equal to one.
These notions were used for instance to prove delocalization via optimal
\ell^{p}
bounds of
the eigenvectors of Wigner matrices
[
29
,
28
]
, for non-Hermitian random
matrices
[
56
]
, and for the adjacency matrix of Erdős-Rényi graphs
[
27
]
.
Recently, localization and delocalization of eigenvectors for the adjacency matrix
of
critical
Erdős-Rényi graphs were established in
[
2
]
.

There is a complementary notion of delocalization, known as
no-gaps delocalization
,
which asserts that for any subset
I\subset[N]
, with
|I|
reasonably large, one has

\|\psi\|_{\ell^{2}(I)}\gtrsim|I|
(again allowing for logarithmic factors). Recently, such delocalizations
have been established for Wigner matrices and matrices with independent and identically
distributed (i.i.d.) entries (cf.
[
56
,
43
,
45
]
).

In the field of
quantum chaos

[
59
]
, in the setting of Hermitian
pseudo-differential operators,
localization and delocalization of normalized eigenvectors are
studied via their associated

semiclassical defect measures
.
Translated to the matrix setting
[
5
]
, we note that
\sum_{x=1}^{N}|\psi(x)|^{2}\delta_{x}
, where
\delta_{x}
denotes the Dirac measure at
x
,
defines a probability measure. One says that
quantum ergodicity

occurs when
\sum_{x=1}^{N}a(x)|\psi(x)|^{2}
is close to
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x=1}^{N}a(x)

for most eigenvectors
\psi
, and
uniquely quantum ergodicity
occurs when this holds
for all eigenvectors. In contrast,
scarring
occurs when we have concentration of the form

\sum_{x\in\Lambda}|\psi(x)|^{2}\geqslant 1-\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon>0
, of the eigenvector
on some small set
\Lambda
. On the other hand, if
\|\psi\|_{p}\asymp N^{f(p)}
for
some
f(p)\neq 1/2-1/p
, then the eigenstate
\psi
is termed to be
non-ergodic
and

multi-fractal

[
44
]
. These notions were recently applied to
the study
of the
eigenfunctions of the discrete Laplacian on large regular graphs
[
3
,
4
]
, and to the proof of
delocalization of eigenvectors of generalized Wigner matrices
[
16
]
.
See also
[
10
]
for results on deformed Wigner matrices.

In this paper we prove that the eigenvectors of non-selfadjoint Toeplitz matrices subject
to small random perturbations localize on a set of cardinality
N/\log N
in the sense that
they scar on a set of size
N/\log N
with probability close to one.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first instance where localization results are
proved in the setting of noisy perturbations of non-Hermitian matrices.

It will be seen below that for eigenpairs
(\lambda,\psi)
,
the length of the localizing
set for
\psi
and the rate of decay of the slowest decaying
pure state
associated
to
\lambda
have the same order of magnitude (see Remark
9.4
). On the
other hand, pure states can be related to the
Lyapunov spectra
of the associated

transfer matrices
(cf.
[
7
, Section 1.2]
).
Therefore, the reader may note that our result on the localization has the same flavor
as the one predicted in the case of the
random Schrödinger operator
on a strip, where
it is conjectured that the rate of decay of eigenfunctions is neither slower nor faster than
the one prescribed by the slowest
Lyapunov exponent
(see
[
33
]
and the
references therein).

1.2.
The results

Let
N_{\pm}\in\mathds{Z}
be such that
-N_{-}\leqslant N_{+}
and either

N_{+}\neq 0
or
N_{-}\neq 0
.
Let
a_{i}\in\mathds{C}
,
i\in\mathds{Z}
, be such that
a_{N_{+}}\neq 0
,
a_{-N_{-}}\neq 0
, and
a_{i}=0

for
i\not\in[-N_{-},N_{+}]
.
Introduce the symbol
p(\zeta)=\sum_{-N_{-}}^{N_{+}}a_{j}\zeta^{-j}
and
the associated

N\times N

Toeplitz
matrix
P_{N}
with entries

P_{N}(i,j)=a_{i-j}
, that is

(1.1)

P_{N}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&a_{-1}&\dots&a_{-N_{-}}&\dots\\
a_{1}&a_{0}&a_{-1}&\dots&\dots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{N_{+}}&\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\\
\dots&\dots&a_{N_{+}}&\dots&a_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

(We refer to Section
4
for an introduction to
the terminology, especially with respect to symbols.)

We consider in this paper noisy perturbations of
P_{N}
of the form

(1.2)

P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}=P_{N}+N^{-\gamma}Q_{N},\quad\gamma>1,

with
Q_{N}
an
N\times N
(random) matrix satisfying Assumptions

1.1
and
1.2
below. The first assumption is on the
existence of finite moments.

Assumption 1.1
.

Let
\{Q_{i,j}\}_{i,j=1}^{N}
be the entries of the
N\times N
noise matrix
Q=Q_{N}
.

(i)

The entries of
Q
are jointly independent and have zero mean.

(ii)

For any
h\in\mathds{N}
there exists an absolute constant
\mathfrak{C}_{h}<\infty
such that

\max_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathds{E}[|Q_{i,j}|^{2h}]\leqslant\mathfrak{C}_{h}.

For notation convenience, we take the sequence
\mathfrak{C}_{h}
increasing in
h
.
To introduce the second assumption, recall Lévy’s concentration function, defined
for any complex-valued random variable
\mathds{X}
and
\varepsilon>0
by

(1.3)

\mathcal{L}(\mathds{X},\varepsilon)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sup_{w\in\mathds{C}}\mathbf{P}(|\mathds{X}-w|\leqslant\varepsilon).

Assumption 1.2
.

Assume that there exist absolute constants
\upeta\in(0,{1}]
and
C_{\ref {assump:anticonc}}<\infty
, such that

(1.4)

{\mathcal{L}(Q_{i,j},\varepsilon)\leqslant C_{\ref {assump:anticonc}}\varepsilon^{1+\upeta}},

for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, uniformly for all
N
and

i,j\in\{1,2,\ldots,N\}
.

(The standard example of a noise matrix satisfying Assumptions

1.1
and
1.2
is the complex Ginibre matrix,
i.e. with i.i.d. entries that are standard complex Gaussian variables.)

It was recently shown in
[
7
,
8
,
64
,
65
]
that all
but
o(N)
of the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}^{N}\}

of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
lie in a small neighborhood
of the curve
p(S^{1})
, where
S^{1}:=\{z\in\mathds{C}:|z|=1\}
; in fact, it was shown
in those references that
the empirical measure of eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
,

(1.5)

L_{N}:=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\lambda_{i}^{N}},

converges weakly
to the push forward of the uniform measure on
S^{1}
by
p
. As part of our
study, we will obtain more precise information, and show (see Theorem

1.5
and Sections
5
-
7
)
that most of the
eigenvalues lie in certain neighborhoods of width of order
\log N/N

that are separated from
p(S^{1})
by distance of the same order.

Our goal in this paper is to study the eigenvectors associated with
the latter (random) eigenvalues. Roughly speaking, we will show that those
we will show that those eigenvalues
\hat{z}
away from certain isolated bad points
of
p(S^{1})
have corresponding eigenvectors which are close to a random linear
combination of the eigenvectors
e_{j}
of
(P_{N}-\hat{z}I)^{*}(P_{N}-\hat{z}I)
associated
with its smallest eigenvalues.
In particular we will show that this random linear
combination of vectors localizes at scale
N/\log N
.
To state our results precisely requires the introduction of some machinery, which we
now do.

Sometimes, the symbol
p
possesses a natural contraction,
defined as follows. Set

(1.6)

{\sf g}(p):={\rm gcd}\{|j|:j\neq 0\text{ and }a_{j}\neq 0\}.

If
{\sf g}_{0}:={\sf g}(p)>1
then
p(\zeta)=q_{p}(\zeta^{{\sf g}_{0}})

for some Laurent polynomial
q_{p}
. If
{\sf g}_{0}=1
then
q_{p}=p
. For
\varepsilon>0

and a set
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathds{C}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\varepsilon}
denotes the
\varepsilon
-blow up of
\mathcal{B}
, that is
the Minkowski sum of the sets
\mathcal{B}
and
D(0,\varepsilon)
, the open disc of radius

\varepsilon
centered at zero.

Definition 1.3

(Set of bad points)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
be the collection of self intersection points
of
q_{p}(S^{1})
, and let

\mathcal{B}_{2}
be the set of branch points,
i.e. points
z

where the Laurent polynomial
p(\cdot)-z
has double roots. Set
\mathcal{B}_{p}:=\mathcal{B}_{1}\cup\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
\mathcal{G}_{p,\varepsilon}:=p(S^{1})\setminus\mathcal{B}_{p}^{\varepsilon}
.

In Definition
1.3
, a point
w\in\mathds{C}
is a self intersection point of
q_{p}(S^{1})

if there exist
\zeta_{1}\neq\zeta_{2}\in S^{1}
so that
q_{p}(\zeta_{1})=q_{p}(\zeta_{2})=w
.

Throughout the paper, we make the following assumption on the symbol
p
.

Assumption 1.4
.

The symbol
p
satisfies

a_{-N_{-}},a_{N_{+}}\neq 0
, and
\mathcal{B}_{1}
is a finite set.

Under Assumption
1.4
,
\mathcal{B}_{p}
is a finite set. Indeed,
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is precisely
the set of all
z
’s such that the discriminant of the polynomial

\zeta\mapsto\zeta^{N_{+}}p(\zeta)-z
vanishes, and
[
15
, Lemma 11.4]
yields that

\mathcal{B}_{2}
is a finite set. We note that by
[
40
]
, unless
N_{-}=N_{+}
and

|a_{-N_{-}}|=|a_{N_{+}}|
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
has cardinality bounded above by
(N_{+}+N_{-}-1)^{2}
, so
symbols avoiding this situation satisfy Assumption
1.4
. For
z\in\mathds{C}
let

d(z)={\rm ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)

denote the winding number of the curve
p(S^{1})
around
z
.
We now describe the collection of eigenvalues of interest to us.
For
0<\varepsilon,C<\infty
and
N
large enough so that
2C\log N/N<\varepsilon
, set

(1.7)

\Omega(\varepsilon,C,N):=\{z\in\mathds{C}:C^{-1}\log N/N<{\mathrm{dist}}(z,\mathcal{G}_{p,\varepsilon})<C\log N/N,d(z)\neq 0\},

where for a set
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathds{C}
and
w\in\mathds{C}
we denote
{\mathrm{dist}}(w,\mathcal{B}):=\inf_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}|w-w^{\prime}|
.
Let
\mathcal{N}_{\Omega(\varepsilon,C,N),N,\gamma}:=|\{\lambda_{i}^{N}\in\Omega(\varepsilon,C,N)\}|

denote the number of eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
that lie in
\Omega(\varepsilon,C,N)
.

The following theorem, a combination of the convergence of
L_{N}
discussed above and
Theorems
5.4
,
5.7
, and
7.1
below, shows that

most
eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
lie in
\Omega(\varepsilon,C,N)
for appropriate
\varepsilon,C
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.4
hold. Fix
\mu>0
and
\gamma>1
.
Then there exist
0<\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}},C_{\ref {theo-location}}<\infty
(depending on
\gamma,\mu
and
p
only)
so that

(1.8)

\mathbf{P}\big(\mathcal{N}_{\Omega(\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}},C_{\ref {theo-location}},N),N,\gamma}<(1-\mu)N\big)\to_{N\to\infty}0.

Theorem
1.5
may be of independent interest
since it improves upon previous
results
[
7
,
8
,
64
,
65
]
by providing a much sharper estimate
on the position of
the eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
. We refer the reader to Sections

5
–
7
for its proof. In what follows, we fix
\mu>0
,
\gamma>1
and consider the

\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}}
and
C_{\ref {theo-location}}
determined by Theorem
1.5
. We then consider
eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}^{N}\in\Omega(\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}},C_{\ref {theo-location}},N)
. By Theorem

1.5
,
most
eigenvalues are of this type. Notice also that for any
z\in\Omega(\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}},C_{\ref {theo-location}},N)
, we have that
d=d(z)\neq 0
.

Figure 1.
Eigenvectors (left panel) and eigenvalues (right panel) for

N=4000
,
\gamma=1.2
and symbol
\zeta+\zeta^{2}
.
Plotted are the moduli of the entries of the eigenvector that
corresponds to the eigenvalue marked with a red
\times
.
The top two rows correspond to situations covered by Theorem

1.6
;
note the localization, which occurs at scale
N/\log N
.
The bottom row is not covered by Theorem
1.6
, because
the chosen eigenvalue is at vanishing distance from
\mathcal{B}_{1}
.

The main result of this paper is the following description of the
(right) eigenvectors of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Fix
\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}},C_{\ref {theo-location}}
and the notation as above.

1. The following occurs with probability approaching one as
N\to\infty
.
For each
\hat{z}\in\Omega(\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}},C_{\ref {theo-location}},N)
which is an eigenvalue
of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
, let
v=v(\hat{z})
denote the corresponding (right)
eigenvector, normalized so that
\|v\|_{2}=1
. Then there
exists a vector
w
with

\|w\|_{2}=1
such that

(1.9)

\|v-w\|_{2}=o(1),

and a constant
c>0
, depending on
\gamma
, so that for any
\ell\in[N]
,

(1.10)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\|w\|_{\ell^{2}([\ell,N])}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-c\ell\log N/N}/c,&\mbox{\rm if $d>0$},\\
\|w\|_{\ell^{2}([1,N-\ell])}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-c\ell\log N/N}/c,&\mbox{\rm if $d<0$}.\end{array}

The vector
w
can be taken as a (random) linear combination of the

|d|
eigenvectors of
(P_{N}-\hat{z}I)^{*}(P_{N}-\hat{z}I)
corresponding to the

|d|
smallest eigenvalues.

2. Fix
z_{0}=z_{0}(N)\in\Omega(\varepsilon_{\ref {theo-location}},C_{\ref {theo-location}},N)
deterministic,

C_{0}
,
\widetilde{C}_{0}
large, and
\eta>0
small. Then, there exist constants

c_{1}=c_{1}(\eta,C_{0},\widetilde{C}_{0})
and
c_{0}=c_{0}(\gamma)\in(0,1)
,
with
c_{0}\to 1
as
\gamma\to 1
and
c_{0}\to 0
as
\gamma\to\infty
, so that, with probability
at least
1-\eta
, for every
\hat{z}=\lambda_{i}^{N}\in D(z_{0},C_{0}\log N/N)
, any

0<\ell\leqslant\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{0}N/\log N
satisfying
\ell^{\prime}-\ell>N^{c_{0}}
and all large
N
,

(1.11)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\|w\|_{\ell^{2}([\ell,\ell^{\prime}])}^{2}\geqslant c_{1}(\ell^{\prime}-\ell)\log N/N,&\mbox{\rm if $d>0$},\\
\|w\|_{\ell^{2}([N-\ell^{\prime},N-\ell])}^{2}\geqslant c_{1}(\ell^{\prime}-\ell)\log N/N,&\mbox{\rm if $d<0$}.\end{array}

Further, for any
0<c^{\prime}\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{0}
,

(1.12)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\|v\|_{\ell^{2}([1,c^{\prime}N/\log N])}^{2}\geqslant c^{\prime}c_{1}/2,&\mbox{\rm if $d>0$},\\
\|v\|_{\ell^{2}([N-c^{\prime}N/\log N,N])}^{2}\geqslant c^{\prime}c_{1}/2,&\mbox{\rm if $d<0$}.\end{array}

Theorem
1.6
shows a
localization
phenomenon,
numerically illustrated in the examples of Figure
1
:
for all eigenvalues in the good regions, the corresponding
eigenvectors localize at scale
N/\log N
, and
for most eigenvalues, this is the scale at which the
eigenvector is “spread out”.
(Contrast the situation with the regime
\gamma<1
, where
delocalization is observed in simulations, see Figure
2
;
we discuss predictions for that regime in Section
3.1
,
after we introduce relevant notions and in particular the relevant

Grushin problem
.)

Building on Theorem
1.6
, equipped with a local estimate
on the number of eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}^{N}
in regions of diameters
O(\log N/N)

(see Theorem
7.4
) and applying a Fubini type argument,
one can show that except for an arbitrarily small fraction of the eigenvalues,
the corresponding eigenvectors
v(\lambda_{i}^{N})
localize at
scale
N/\log N
. In particular, we prove the following result.

Corollary 1.7
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.4
hold. Then, for
any
\mu>0
there exists
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}>0
so that
with
|\mathrm{supp\,}_{\mu_{1}}(v)|:=\min\{|I|:\|v\|_{\ell^{2}(I)}>1-\mu_{1}\}
,

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbf{E}\#\{i:\mathrm{supp\,}_{\mu_{1}}(v(\lambda_{i}^{N}))<\mu_{2}N/\log N\}\leqslant\mu.

Figure 2.
Eigenvectors (left panel) and eigenvalues (right panel) for

N=4000
,
\gamma=0.8
and symbol
\zeta+\zeta^{2}
.
These cases are not covered by Theorem
1.6
.
Note the stark difference with the corresponding situations in Figure

1
, in both the location of eigenvalues relative to
p(S^{1})
and
in the localization properties of eigenvectors.

Theorem
1.6
states that the eigenvectors of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
corresponding
to most eigenvalues
\hat{z}
can be approximated by a random linear combination
of the eigenvectors
e_{j}
of
(P_{N}-\hat{z}I)^{*}(P_{N}-\hat{z}I)
associated with its
|d|
smallest
eigenvalues. These are precisely the right singular vectors of
P_{N}-\hat{z}I
associated with
its
|d|
smallest singular values.
However, these singular vectors
e_{j}
are (in general) difficult objects to study and
do not admit an easy description.
Therefore, we will
approximate these singular vectors with certain
quasimodes
of
the operator

P_{N}
. The term quasimode for
P_{N}
and a (quasi-)eigenvalue
z
refers to
a approximate

\ell^{2}
-normalized eigenvectors
\psi\in\mathds{C}^{N}
of
P_{N}-z
in the sense that

\|(P_{N}-z)\psi\|\to 0,\quad N\to\infty.

In the literature quasimodes are also referred to as

pseudo-eigenvectors

or
pseudomodes
. In Section
9
, we describe
a
|d|
-dimensional
space of quasimodes associated with
P_{N}-zI
, and in Section
10.2
, we show
that the eigenvectors
e_{j}
of
(P_{N}-zI)^{*}(P_{N}-zI)
associated with its
|d|
smallest
eigenvalues are close to these quasimodes. We refer to Propositions

9.6
and
10.3
for the precise construction, which we do not repeat
here. We emphasize however that the
construction
of the
e_{j}
-s depends on
p
,
N
and

z
only and not on
Q
(even if eventually the value of
z
to which it will be applied will
depend on
Q
).

The upper bound (
1.10
) is due to the decaying nature of these
|d|
linearly independent quasimodes either to the left (when
d<0
) or to the right
(when
d>0
).
These quasimodes decay exponentially quickly,
|u(n)|\asymp\mathrm{e}^{-rn}
or

|u(n)|\asymp\mathrm{e}^{-r(N-n)}
, however, at different rates
r>0
. Out of these
|d|

quasimodes the first
(|d|-{\sf g}_{0})
(recall (
1.6
)) quasimodes decay
at a constant rate
r>0
, resulting in them being completely localized to a point, i.e. either on the left or right hand side of the interval
[1,N]
. In contrast, the rest decay at a rate

r\asymp\log N/N
, which implies that they localize at a scale
N/\log N
. In contrast,
the lower bound (
1.11
) follows upon showing that
w
has a non-negligible
projection (in
\ell^{2}
) onto
\mathcal{S}
, the subspace spanned by the last
{\sf g}_{0}

quasimodes that decay precisely at rate
\log N/N
.

Note that Theorem
1.6
and Corollary
1.7
establish absences
of quantum ergodicity and no-gaps delocalization, and show that the semiclassical
defect measure in this setting is the Dirac measure at zero or one, depending on
whether
d
is positive of negative.

One may wonder whether the assumption
\hat{z}\in D(z_{0},C_{0}\log N/N)

in the second part of Theorem
1.6
is optimal. It will be clear from the proof
that to derive (
1.11
) one needs to control the supremum of the random field

z\mapsto\widetilde{f}(z)^{t}Q\widetilde{e}(z)
for some
\widetilde{e}(z),\widetilde{f}(z)\in\mathds{C}^{N}
such that the
Lipschitz norms of the functions
z\mapsto\widetilde{e}(z)
and
z\mapsto\widetilde{f}(z)
are

O(N/\log N)
and
O(1)
, respectively. It is then standard to check that the supremum of
the field
\{\widetilde{e}(z)^{t}Q\widetilde{f}(z)\}
can only be bounded by an
O(1)
quantity
in discs of radius
O(\log N/N)
.
The boundedness of this random field is crucial in deriving that
w
has non-negligible
projection onto
\mathcal{S}
. Repeating the same reasoning one can also observe that
for all
\hat{z}
simultaneously in the good region, with probability approaching one, the

\ell^{2}
norm of the projection of
w
onto
\mathcal{S}
is at least of the order
\log N/N
, and
we believe that this is the correct picture.

1.3.
Connection to pseudospectra and pseudo-eigenvectors

Roughly speaking, the pseudospectrum of an operator represent the spectrum of the operator when subjected to the worst-case perturbation. However, here we study the spectrum under a
typical perturbation
.
Nevertheless, in many scenarios the spectrum of random perturbations of a non-self-adjoint operator closely resembles the
pseudospectral level lines
of the unperturbed operator (cf.
[
7
, Section 1.3]
and the references therein). There have been attempts to understand pseudospectral properties of Toeplitz matrices. It was proved that the
\varepsilon
-pseudospectrum of an unperturbed Toeplitz matrix converges to an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of the spectrum of the limiting Toeplitz operator
[
55
]
. On the other hand, in
[
14
]
it is shown that for
P_{N}
any
asymptotically good
pseudo-eigenvector must be
asymptotically strongly localized
, meaning that almost all its mass is carried either by the subset
[1,j_{N}]
or the subset
[N-j_{N},N]
for any sequence
\{j_{N}\}
such that
j_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
. Note the contrast with the localization behavior of the eigenvectors of the noisy version of
P_{N}
: they localize with
j_{N}\asymp N/\log N
.

For a more general model, the twisted Toeplitz matrices, a special case of the Berezin-Toeplitz quantization of the two dimensional torus, it has been shown in
[
71
]
that if certain
(anti-)twist condition
is satisfied by the

symbol
of the operator, then the pseudo-eigenvectors are localized in the form of
localized wave packets
. This has been generalized in
[
13
]

to the
Berezin-Toeplitz quantizations of compact symplectic Kähler manifolds.
In the field of microlocal analysis of pseudo-differential operators,
the analogue of this (anti-)twist condition is the
non-vanishing of the Poisson bracket of the real and imaginary parts of the
principal symbol
of the operator. This condition, also known as
Hörmander’s
commutator condition
[
39
]
, is a corner stone in the now classical theory of local (non-)solvability of partial differential equations. In the works
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
76
,
23
,
53
]
it was linked via a WKB construction to the construction of wave packet pseudomodes of Schrödinger operators with complex potentials,
and more generally to nonselfadjoint pseudo-differential operators.

1.4.
Extensions

A natural extension of our results would be to the region
\gamma\in(1/2,1]
, see
Section
3.1
for a discussion.
We mention a couple of additional
potentially interesting extensions, of broad interest.

Sup-norm delocalization versus no-gaps delocalization.
As already mentioned in Section
1.1
,
there are two complementary notions of delocalizations in the random matrix literature. It is shown in
[
11
,
57
]
that both these notions of delocalization hold for Wigner matrices under various assumptions on it entries. There is no reason to believe that these two notions of delocalizations should hold simultaneously in any given setting.

Indeed, from the proof of Theorem
1.6
it follows that when
P_{N}
is a Jordan block, i.e. its symbol is
p(\zeta)=\zeta
, and
\gamma>3/2
we have that
\|v\|_{\infty}\asymp\sqrt{{\log N}/{N}}
for an eigenvector
v
corresponding to a bulk eigenvalue. See Remark
11.2
for further details.
Thus, in this simple setting, the eigenvectors corresponding to most of the eigenvalues are completely delocalized according to the sup-norm criterion. However, they do not satisfy no-gaps delocalization.

It is worth investigating whether one indeed has that
\|v\|_{\infty}\asymp\sqrt{{\log N}/{N}}
for all
\gamma>1
and any finitely banded
P_{N}
.

Localization for the outlier eigenvalues and multi-fractal structure.

Based on simulations and some heuristic arguments,
we predict that the eigenvector
\psi
corresponding to an eigenvalue
residing at a distance of order
N^{\upalpha-1}
,
\upalpha\in(0,1]
,
from the spectral curve localizes
at scale
N^{1-\upalpha}
. This shows in particular
that for such a
\psi
one has

\|\psi\|_{p}\asymp N^{(\upalpha-1)\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})}
,
establishing that such eigenvectors are multi-fractal.
The same reasoning shows that the eigenvectors corresponding to
outlier eigenvalues, i.e. those are at a distance of order one from
p(S^{1})
,
would be completely localized. That is, most of their
mass is carried by finitely many entries.
It seems plausible that the methods of our current work
could be adapted to prove these results.

1.5.
Structure of the paper

In Section
2
, we introduce a Grushin problem for
a matrix
P
, which allows us to represent the null space of
P
,
if non-empty,
in terms of a certain resolvent expansion,
see Lemma
2.1
. Section
3
then provides a
sketch of the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
Section
4
discusses results on Toeplitz and related operators that are used later in the paper. Sections
5
-
7

are devoted to estimates on the location of “most”
eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
, which together yield Theorem

1.5
. The following Section
8

derives estimates on the resolvent of of
P_{N}-zI
, for appropriate
z

in the “good” region. Section
9
is devoted to
the study of quasimodes of Toeplitz matrices, which are the building blocks for the eigenvectors of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
and eventually give the vector
w

in the statement of Theorem
1.6
. Section

10
gives estimates on the singular values and vectors of
P_{N}-z
,
and bounds on various norms of matrices appearing in the Grushin problem.
Finally, Section
11
collects all preparatory material and provides the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

1.6.
Notation

We use the following set of notation throughout this paper. The notation

a\ll b
means that

Ca\leqslant b
for some sufficiently large constant
C>0
.
Writing
a\asymp b
means
that there exists a constant
C>1
such that
C^{-1}a\leqslant b\leqslant Ca
. The notation

a\gtrsim b
is used to denote that
a\geqslant C^{-1}b
, while we write
a=o(b)
to denote

a\leqslant C^{-1}b
for
all

C<\infty
. Constants
C,\widetilde{C}>0

denoted explicitly in the proofs may change value from line to line,
while constants such as
C_{\ref {assump:anticonc}}
will be kept fixed throughout.
The notation
f=O(N)

means that there exists a constant
C>0
(independent of
N
) such that
|f|\leqslant CN
.
When we want to emphasize that the constant
C>0
depends on some parameter

k
, then we write
C_{k}
, or with the above notation
O_{k}(N)
.

We use the standard notation of ceiling and floor: for
x\in\mathds{R}
we write
\lceil x\rceil:=\max\{n\in\mathds{Z};n\geqslant x\}
and
\lfloor x\rfloor:=\max\{n\in\mathds{Z};n\leqslant x\}
, respectively. For
m,n\in\mathds{Z}
the notation
[m,n]
is used to denote the discrete interval
\{m,m+1,\ldots,n\}
and we use the shorthand
[n]
to denote the discrete interval
[1,n]
.

We identify
\ell^{2}(K)\simeq\ell^{2}_{K}:=\{u\in\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z});\mathrm{supp\,}u\subset K\}

and we will frequently identify
\ell^{2}_{[0,N-1]}\simeq\mathds{C}^{N}

so the norm
\|\cdot\|
and Hermitian scalar product
\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
on
\mathds{C}^{N}

will be the ones of
\ell^{2}_{[0,N-1]}
. When the Euclidean norm and inner products are considered over some specific discrete interval
I
we write
\|\cdot\|_{\ell^{2}(I)}
and
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle_{\ell^{2}(I)}
. The notation
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is used to denote the supremum norm for a vector. For a matrix
A
the notations
\|A\|
and
\|A\|_{{\rm HS}}
denote its operator norm, and Hilbert-Schmidt norms, respectively. For a vector
v\in\mathds{C}^{N}
we write
v^{*}\in(\mathds{C}^{N})^{*}
for its natural dual with respect to the Hermitian scalar product, i.e.

v^{*}u=\langle u|v\rangle
for any
v\in\mathds{C}^{N}
.

For a
N\times N
matrix
A
we denote by

t_{1}(A)\leqslant\dots\leqslant t_{N}(A)
the eigenvalues of
\sqrt{A^{*}A}
.
The singular values

s_{1}(A)\geqslant\cdots\geqslant s_{N}(A)
of
A
are then given by

(1.13)

s_{N-n+1}(A)=t_{n}(A),\quad n=1,\dots,N.

For brevity, we sometimes write
s_{\max}(A)
and
s_{\min}(A)
for the maximum and the minimum singular values of
A
. We let
I_{d}
denote the identity matrix, viewed as element in
\mathds{C}^{d\times d}
.

The following standard conventions are followed:

a\lor b\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\max\{a,b\},\penalty\ a,b\in\mathds{R},\qquad\prod_{1}^{0}a_{j}=1,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sum_{1}^{0}a_{j}=0.

We also use the Dirac notation

\delta_{n,m}={\bf I}(m=n)
.
The notation
\Theta(\cdot)
denotes the Heaviside function, that is
\Theta(x)=0
when
x\leqslant 0
, and
\Theta(x)=1
for
x>0
.
For
z\in\mathds{C}
and
r>0
, we write
D(z,r)\subset\mathds{C}

for the (open) disc of radius
r
centered at
z
. For
\varepsilon>0
and a set
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathds{C}
,

\mathcal{B}^{\varepsilon}
denotes the
\varepsilon
-blow up of the set
\mathcal{B}
, that is
the Minkowski sum of the sets
\mathcal{B}
and
D(0,\varepsilon)
. The notation
\mathcal{B}\dot{\cup}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is
used to denote a disjoint union. For a set
D\subset\mathds{C}
we use both
{\rm cl}(D)
and
\overline{D}
to denote its closure.
The complement of an event
\mathcal{A}
is denoted
\mathcal{A}^{\complement}
.

2.
The Grushin Problems

We begin by setting up, in some generality, a well-posed Grushin problem,
based on
[
73
,
36
]
, see also
[
65
,
64
]
. It, and its
behavior under perturbations, will play a crucial role in our analysis.

Roughly speaking, the Grushin problem amounts to replacing an operator of
interest by an enlarged bijective system. In the context of
linear partial differential equations, the
study of such enlarged system of operators
can be traced back to Grushin
[
35
]
,
where it was used to study hypoelliptic
operators. In a different setting, such an enlarged system was used
by Sjöstrand

[
61
]
, whose notation we use.
It has also been quite useful in bifurcation
theory, numerical analysis, and for treatments of spectral problems arising in
electromagnetism and quantum mechanics. See the review paper
[
67
]
.

2.1.
Grushin problem for the unperturbed operator

Let
P
be a complex
N\times N
-matrix.
(In our application, we will often take
P=P_{N}-zI
where
P_{N}
is
the (deterministic)
Toeplitz
matrix with symbol
p
and
z
is a
random
parameter close to the
spectral curve
p(S^{1})
. Then, all objects implicitly depend on
z
,
and we supress this dependence in notation when not needed.)
Let

(2.1)

0\leqslant t_{1}^{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant t_{N}^{2}

denote the eigenvalues of
P^{*}P
with associated orthonormal basis
of eigenvectors
e_{1},\dots,e_{N}\in\mathds{C}^{N}
. The spectra of
P^{*}P

and
PP^{*}
are equal and we can find an orthonormal basis

f_{1},\dots,f_{N}\in\mathds{C}^{N}
of eigenvectors of
PP^{*}
associated with the
eigenvalues (
2.1
) such that

(2.2)

P^{*}f_{i}=t_{i}e_{i},\quad Pe_{i}=t_{i}f_{i},\quad i=1,\dots,N.

Let
0<\alpha\ll 1
and let
M>0
be the number of singular values
t_{i}\in[0,\alpha]
, i.e.

(2.3)

0\leqslant t_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant t_{M}\leqslant\alpha<t_{M+1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant t_{N}.

Let
\delta_{i}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant M
, denote an orthonormal basis of
\mathds{C}^{M}
.
Put

(2.4)

R_{+}:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\delta_{i}\circ e_{i}^{*},\quad R_{-}:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}f_{i}\circ\delta_{i}^{*},

Then the Grushin problem

(2.5)

\mathcal{P}:=\begin{pmatrix}P&R_{-}\\
R_{+}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}:\mathds{C}^{N}\times\mathds{C}^{M}\longrightarrow\mathds{C}^{N}\times\mathds{C}^{M}

is bijective. To see this we take
(v,v_{+})\in\mathds{C}^{N}\times\mathds{C}^{M}
and proceed to solve

(2.6)

\mathcal{P}\begin{pmatrix}u\\
u_{-}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}v\\
v_{+}\end{pmatrix}.

We write
u=\sum_{1}^{N}u(j)e_{j}
and
v=\sum_{1}^{N}v(j)f_{j}
. Similarly, we express

u_{-},v_{+}
in the basis
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{M}
.
The relation (
2.2
) then shows
that (
2.6
) is equivalent to

\begin{cases}\sum_{1}^{N}t_{i}u(i)f_{i}+\sum_{1}^{M}u_{-}(j)f_{j}=\sum_{1}^{N}v(j)f_{j},\\
u(j)=v_{+}(j),\quad j=1,\dots,M,\end{cases}

which can be written as

(2.7)

\begin{cases}\qquad\quad t_{i}u(i)=v(i),\qquad\qquad\qquad i=M+1,\ldots,N,\\[6.45831pt]
\begin{pmatrix}t_{i}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u(i)\\
u_{-}(i)\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}v(i)\\
v_{+}(i)\end{pmatrix},\quad i=1,\dots,M.\end{cases}

Since

\begin{pmatrix}t_{i}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&-t_{i}\\
\end{pmatrix},

we see that

(2.8)

\mathcal{P}^{-1}=:\mathcal{E}=\begin{pmatrix}E&E_{+}\\
E_{-}&E_{-+}\\
\end{pmatrix}

with

(2.9)

\begin{split}&E=\sum_{M+1}^{N}\frac{1}{t_{i}}e_{i}\circ f_{i},\quad E_{+}=\sum_{1}^{M}e_{i}\circ\delta_{i}^{*},\quad E_{-}=\sum_{1}^{M}\delta_{i}\circ f_{i}^{*},\quad\text{ and }\quad E_{-+}=-\sum_{1}^{M}t_{j}\delta_{j}\circ\delta_{j}^{*}.\end{split}

From (
2.3
) and (
2.9
) it follows that we have the following norm estimates

(2.10)

\|E(z)\|\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha},\quad\|E_{\pm}\|=1,\quad\|E_{-+}\|\leqslant\alpha.

Next, we recall a general fact on well-posed Grushin problems.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an
N
-dimensional complex Hilbert space, and
let
N\geqslant M>0
. Suppose that

\mathcal{P}=\begin{pmatrix}P&R_{-}\\
R_{+}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathds{C}^{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}\times\mathds{C}^{M}

is a bijective matrix of linear operators, with inverse

\mathcal{E}=\begin{pmatrix}E&E_{+}\\
E_{-}&E_{-+}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Then,
E_{+}:\mathcal{N}(E_{-+})\to\mathcal{N}(P)
is bijective with inverse

R_{+}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathcal{N}(P)}
, and

E_{-}^{*}:\mathcal{N}(E_{-+}^{*})\to\mathcal{N}(P^{*})
is bijective with inverse

R_{-}^{*}\!\upharpoonright_{\mathcal{N}(P^{*})}
.

Proof.

From
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{E}=1
, we get that
PE_{+}+R_{-}E_{-+}=0
and so

(2.11)

E_{+}:\mathcal{N}(E_{-+})\to\mathcal{N}(P).

Similarly, we get from
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{P}=1
the equation
E_{-}P+E_{-+}R_{+}=0
, and hence

(2.12)

R_{+}:\mathcal{N}(P)\to\mathcal{N}(E_{-+}).

The identity
EP+E_{+}R_{+}=1
yields that
E_{+}R_{+}=1
on
\mathcal{N}(P)
,
which, together with
R_{+}E_{+}=1
, shows that (
2.11
), (
2.12
), are
bijective and inverser to each other.
The proof of the second claim is similar, one can follow the same arguments applied to

\mathcal{P}^{*}\mathcal{E}^{*}=\mathcal{E}^{*}\mathcal{P}^{*}=1
.
∎

2.2.
Grushin problem for the perturbed operator

Now we turn to the perturbed operator

(2.13)

P^{\delta}:=P+\delta Q,\quad 0\leqslant\delta\ll 1.

where
Q
is a complex
N\times N
-matrix (eventually, random).
Let
R_{\pm}
be
as in (
2.4
), and put

(2.14)

\mathcal{P}^{\delta}:=\begin{pmatrix}P^{\delta}&R_{-}\\
R_{+}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}:\mathds{C}^{N}\times\mathds{C}^{M}\longrightarrow\mathds{C}^{N}\times\mathds{C}^{M}

with
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{0}
. Applying
\mathcal{E}
(see (
2.8
)) from
the right to (
2.14
) yields

(2.15)

\mathcal{P}^{\delta}\mathcal{E}=I_{N+M}+\begin{pmatrix}\delta QE&\delta QE_{+}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Suppose that
(I+\delta QE)
is invertible. It is then
straightforward to check that
\mathcal{P}^{\delta}
is invertible, with
inverse

(2.16)

(\mathcal{P}^{\delta})^{-1}=:\mathcal{E}^{\delta}=\begin{pmatrix}E^{\delta}&E^{\delta}_{+}\\
E^{\delta}_{-}&E^{\delta}_{-+}\end{pmatrix},

where

(2.17)

E^{\delta}=E(I+\delta QE)^{-1},\quad E_{-}^{\delta}=E_{-}(I+\delta QE)^{-1},

(2.18)

E^{\delta}_{-+}=E_{-+}-E_{-}(I+\delta QE)^{-1}\delta QE_{+},

and

(2.19)

E^{\delta}_{+}=E_{+}-E(I+\delta QE)^{-1}\delta QE_{+}.

We note that if one takes
P=P_{N}-zI_{N}
with
z
an eigenvalue of
P_{N}+\delta Q
, then Lemma
2.1
applied to
P^{\delta}
gives a
convenient description of the null-space of
P
, which is precisely
the eigenspace of
P_{N}+\delta Q
corresponding to the eigenvalue
z
.
This observation will be a crucial part of our analysis, see (
3.1
) below.

Remark 2.2
.

Under the additional assumption that

(2.20)

2\delta\|Q\|\alpha^{-1}\leqslant 1,

which will occur in our setup of
Q
as in Assumption

1.1
if
\delta=N^{-\gamma}
with
\gamma>3/2

(and with
\alpha\,\asymp\,N^{-1}
),
we obtain by a Neumann series argument that

(2.21)

\mathcal{E}^{\delta}=\begin{pmatrix}E^{\delta}&E^{\delta}_{+}\\
E^{\delta}_{-}&E^{\delta}_{-+}\end{pmatrix}=\mathcal{E}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-\delta)^{n}\begin{pmatrix}E(QE)^{n}&(EQ)^{n}E_{+}\\
E_{-}(QE)^{n}&E_{-}(QE)^{n-1}QE_{+}\\
\end{pmatrix},

where by (
2.20
), (
2.10
),

(2.22)

\begin{split}&\|E^{\delta}\|=\|E(1+\delta QE)^{-1}\|\leqslant 2\|E\|\leqslant 2\alpha^{-1},\\
&\|E_{+}^{\delta}\|=\|(1+\delta QE)^{-1}E_{+}\|\leqslant 2\|E_{+}\|\leqslant 2,\\
&\|E_{-}^{\delta}\|=\|E_{-}(1+\delta QE)^{-1}\|\leqslant 2\|E_{-}\|\leqslant 2,\\
&\|E_{-+}^{\delta}-E_{-+}\|=\|E_{-}(1+\delta QE)^{-1}\delta QE_{+}\|\leqslant 2\|\delta Q\|\leqslant\alpha.\\
\end{split}

In particular, in that case,

(2.23)

E_{+}^{\delta}=E_{+}-\delta QE(1+O(\delta\|Q\|\alpha^{-1}))E_{+}

and

(2.24)

E_{-+}^{\delta}=E_{-+}-\delta E_{-}(1+O(\delta\|Q\|\alpha^{-1}))QE_{+}.

3.
Structure of the proof of Theorem
1.6

A key ingredient for the proof Theorem
1.6
is Theorem
1.5
. The proof of the latter result splits into two parts: In the first part we show that all eigenvalues must be separated from
p(S^{1})
by a distance of the order
\log N/N
. At a very high level it involves an expansion of the determinant of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}-zI_{N}
, with
z\in\mathds{C}
, identifying the dominant term in that expansion, and showing that the dominant cannot be equal to zero (with probability approaching one) when
z
is in the vicinity of the spectral curve. We refer the reader to Section
5
-
6
for further details on these steps. The second part of Theorem
1.5
requires us to show that most of the eigenvalues must be within a distance
O(\log N/N)
from spectral curve, again with probability approaching one. This is achieved by an application of Jensen’s formula together with upper and lower bounds on the log-potential of
L_{N}
(see (
1.5
)). See Section
7
for details.

In the remainder of this section we describe the structure of the proof
of Theorem
1.6
,
taking for granted Theorem
1.5
and various
technical estimates.
The proof of Theorem
1.6

begins with the Grushin problem
for
P^{\delta}_{z}=P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}-zI
, see (
2.16
),
for
\delta=N^{-\gamma}
,

z
which is
roughly an eigenvalue, and
M=|{\rm ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)|
(this will lead to

t_{M+1}\,\gtrsim\,\log N/N
and

\alpha
bounded below by a constant multiple of
\log N/N
, see Proposition
10.1
).
To keep track of the dependence on
z
, throughout this section we write
E(z),E_{+}(z)
, etc.
To
relate the null-space of
P^{\delta}_{z}
with the null space of
E^{\delta}_{-+}(z)
we will use
Lemma
2.1
in an indirect manner: As in its proof note that from (
2.14
)
and (
2.16
),

(3.1)

E^{\delta}(z)P^{\delta}_{z}+E_{+}^{\delta}(z)R_{+}(z)=I\quad\text{ and }\quad E_{-}^{\delta}(z)P_{z}^{\delta}+E_{-+}^{\delta}(z)R_{+}(z)=0.

If
z=\hat{z}
were an eigenvalue of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
with corresponding normalized eigenvector

v

then, with notation
as in Section
2
and recalling the definition of
E_{+}^{\delta}(z)
,
we would obtain from (
3.1
) that
since
\{e_{i}(\hat{z})\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
\mathds{C}^{N}
,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{i=M+1}^{N}(e_{i}(\hat{z})^{*}v)\cdot e_{i}(\hat{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I-E_{+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z}))v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(I-E_{+}^{\delta}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z}))v-E(\hat{z})(I+\delta QE(\hat{z}))^{-1}\delta QE_{+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(\hat{z})(I+\delta QE(\hat{z}))^{-1}\delta QE_{+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})v.

Consider first the case where
\gamma
is large (
\gamma>3/2
will do). Since

\|Q\|=O(N^{1/2+\upepsilon})
, for any
\upepsilon>0
, with high probability, we obtain that
N^{-\gamma}\|Q\|\alpha^{-1}=o(1)
and therefore (
2.20
) holds.
Using then (
2.23
)-(
2.24
),
the projection of
v
on
\mbox{\rm span}(e_{i}(z),i\geqslant M+1)
is negligible,
which yields
the first
part of Theorem
1.6
.

To see the second part, still in the case of
large
\gamma
(here we will need
\gamma>2
) and
z=\hat{z}
, we obtain from (
3.1
) that

(3.3)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-E_{-+}^{\delta}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})v=-E_{-+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})v+\delta E_{-}(\hat{z})QE_{+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})x

\displaystyle-\delta^{2}E_{-}(\hat{z})(I+\delta QE(\hat{z}))^{-1}QE(\hat{z})QE_{+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})v,

where we also have used the resolvent expansion. By the same reasoning as above, the third term in (
3.3
)
turns out to be of order

o(\delta)
, hence negligible compared to the first two terms. Therefore, recalling the definitions of
E_{\pm}(z),E_{-+}(z)
, and
R_{+}(z)
we obtain that,
with
a_{j}=(e_{j}(\hat{z})^{*}v)
,

(3.4)

-E_{-+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})v+\delta E_{-}(\hat{z})QE_{+}(\hat{z})R_{+}(\hat{z})v\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{M}a_{i}t_{i}\delta_{i}+\delta\sum_{i=1}^{M}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{M}a_{j}(f_{i}(\hat{z})^{*}Qe_{j}(\hat{z}))\right]\delta_{i}=o(\delta).

Now, again by Proposition
10.1
, there exists
M>M_{0}\geqslant 0
so that
t_{j}
decay exponentially in
N
for
j\in[M_{0}]
. Thus,
we obtain from (
3.4
)
that for
\gamma
large,

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{M_{0}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{M}a_{j}\cdot(f_{i}(\hat{z})^{*}Qe_{j}(\hat{z}))\right]\delta_{i}\right\|=o(1).

Assume now that
a_{j}=o(1)
for
j=M_{0}+1,\ldots,M
. Using a basic chaining argument we would then conclude that

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{M_{0}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{M_{0}}a_{j}\cdot(f_{i}(\hat{z})^{*}Qe_{j}(\hat{z}))\right]\delta_{i}\right\|=\|a^{{\sf T}}A\|=o(1),

where
A
is the
M_{0}\times M_{0}
matrix with entries
A_{i,j}=f_{i}(\hat{z})^{*}Qe_{j}(\hat{z})
. If
\hat{z}
were deterministic,
we would have that
the smallest singular value of
A
is
o(1)

and this would lead to a contradiction.
Since
\hat{z}
is actually random, we will proceed by using the fact that the functions
f_{i},e_{i}
are localized, which makes the minimal singular value of
A
continuous in
z
.

When
\gamma\in(1,2]
, we cannot use in (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) an a-priori bound of the form (
2.20
). Instead, we use a lower bound on the minimum singular
value of
I+\delta QE
, see (
10.10
), and the resolvent expansion to replace
(I+\delta QE)^{-1}
by

\sum_{i=0}^{L}(-\delta QE)^{i}+(-\delta QE)^{L+1}(I-\delta QE)^{-1}
for an appropriate
L
. Proposition
10.11
and
the a-priori bounds on the minimal singular value of
I+\delta QE
suffice to control the sum, and again we use a net in order to work with deterministic
z
’s.

To carry out this program necessitates a fair amount of auxillary results. We need a-priori estimates for the singular values and associated quasimodes of
P_{N}-zI
for
z
close to the location
of eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
. So we begin in Section
4
with a fairly detailed analysis of the singular values and vectors of
P_{N}-zI
(for general
z
), and relate the
singular values to the winding number of
p
around
z
(we will stay away of the bad set
\mathcal{B}_{p}
of Definition
1.3
, so that the winding number is locally constant and
the distance of
z
from
p(S^{1})
is controlled). In Sections

5
-
7
we provide precise estimates for the range of
z
s that we need to consider, that is, for the location of the
eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
. Those precise
estimatess are then used in Section
9
in constructing
the quasimodes of
(P_{N}-zI)(P_{N}-zI)^{*}
, in terms of the roots of the symbol
p-z
. These quasimodes are then used in Section
10
, where we show that the
eigenvectors are appropriate linear combinations of the localized quasimodes.

3.1.
The case
\gamma<1
- discussion and speculations

We end this section with some brief remarks concerning
\gamma<1
.
In that regime, the single entries of
\delta Q=N^{-\gamma}Q
are larger than
N^{-1}
,
and in particular are asymptotically larger than the distance of the eigenvalue
from the spectral curve. In particular, when writing the Grushin problem
(
2.14
), one is forced to take
M
growing with
N
(in fact, essentially

M\,\asymp\,N^{2(1-\gamma)}
; This is forced by the requirement
that
\|E_{-}(\delta Q)E_{+}\|<M/N
). The resulting eigenvector of
P^{\delta}

are expected to
be a combination of the
M
bottom quasimodes, with random coefficients.
Since the quasimodes oscillate at scale
N/M=N^{2\gamma-1}
,
the combination is expected to converge to a
\gamma
-dependent
Gaussian process with
correlation length of that scale. The simulations in Figure
2
are in
line with this picture, although proving it require ideas going beyond the methods of this paper.

4.
Analysis of Toeplitz matrices

In this section we begin with presenting some fundamental results about the calculus of
Toeplitz matrices. In particular we will focus on symbols
given by Laurent polynomials
and we will discuss the quantization procedure which maps such a
symbol to an operator acting on functions on
\mathds{Z}
,
\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z})
,
\ell^{2}([0,\infty[)
, and

\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z})
.

4.1.
Toeplitz matrices

We begin by recalling some well known facts about Toeplitz matrices, see for instance

[
15
]
and the references therein.

For a
u\in\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z})
we define the Fourier transform by

(4.1)

\mathcal{F}u(\xi)=\sum_{n\in\mathds{Z}}u_{n}\mathrm{e}^{-in\xi},\quad\xi\in\mathds{R}/2\pi\mathds{Z},

so that
\mathcal{F}:\ell^{2}(Z)\to L^{2}(\mathds{R}/2\pi\mathds{Z},\frac{d\xi}{2\pi})
is unitary,
and

(4.2)

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f)(n)=f_{n}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathds{R}/2\pi\mathds{Z}}f(\xi)\mathrm{e}^{in\xi}d\xi.

We consider the symbol class of continuous functions on the unit circle
S^{1}

with absolutely convergent Fourier series called the Wiener algebra

(4.3)

W:=\{a\in C(S^{1});\penalty\ \sum_{n\in\mathds{Z}}|a_{n}|<+\infty\}.

Using the Fourier transform we can represent a symbol
p\in W
by

(4.4)

p(\mathrm{e}^{i\xi})=\sum_{n\in\mathds{Z}}p_{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-in\xi},\quad\xi\in\mathds{R}/2\pi\mathds{Z}.

We can
quantize

the symbol
p\in W
by

(4.5)

\mathrm{Op}(p)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\,p\,\mathcal{F}.

Using Parseval’s equality it is easy to see that
p(\tau):\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z})\to\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z})
is a bounded
operator; it is also straight forward to check that
the
\ell^{2}
adjoint of
\mathrm{Op}(p)
is given by

(4.6)

\mathrm{Op}(p)^{*}=\mathrm{Op}(\overline{\widetilde{p}}),\quad\widetilde{p}(\zeta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{p}(1/\zeta).

Let
\tau
denote the right shift operator
(\tau\psi)(n)=\psi(n-1)
on
\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z})
or more
generally on functions
\psi:\mathds{Z}\to\mathds{C}
. Notice that
\tau\mathrm{e}^{in\theta}=\mathrm{e}^{-i\theta}\mathrm{e}^{in\theta}
, so
the symbol of
\tau
is given by
\mathrm{e}^{-i\xi}=1/\zeta
,
\zeta\in S^{1}
. So we can express (
4.5
)
as well as

(4.7)

\mathrm{Op}(p)=\sum_{n\in\mathds{Z}}p_{n}\,\tau^{n}.

which can be seen to act more generally on function
\psi:\mathds{Z}\to\mathds{C}
.

Let
K\subset\mathds{Z}
be a finite subset or an infinite interval. We identify

\ell^{2}(K)\simeq\ell^{2}_{K}:=\{u\in\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z});\mathrm{supp\,}u\subset K\}
, and
we define

(4.8)

P_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}P_{K}(p)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{1}_{K}\mathrm{Op}(p)\mathbf{1}_{K}:\ell^{2}_{K}\to\ell^{2}_{K}.

When
K
is finite we call
P_{K}
a
Toeplitz matrix
and when
K
is infinite,
for example
K=]-\infty,0]
or
K=[0,\infty[
we call
P_{K}
an
infinite Toeplitz matrix
.
For simplicity we will sometimes write
P_{N}=P_{N}(p)
when
K=[0,N-1]
and
P(p)

when
K=[0,\infty[
.

Notice that the matrix elements of
P_{N}
are given by

(4.9)

P_{N}(\nu,\mu)=p_{\nu-\mu},\quad\nu,\mu\in[0,N-1].

A counter part to the Toeplitz matrices are the
Hankel matrices
. Let
R

be the reflection operator defined by
(R\psi)(n)=\psi(-n)
on functions

\psi:\mathds{Z}\to\mathds{C}
. Let

(4.10)

\chi_{n}(\zeta)=\zeta^{n},\qquad\zeta\in S^{1},n\in\mathds{Z}.

For
a\in W
we define the Hankel matrix of
a
by

(4.11)

H(a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{1}^{\infty}a_{n}H(\chi_{n}),\quad H(\chi_{n})=\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty[}\tau^{n-1}R\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty[}.

Represented as infinite matrices we see that Toeplitz matrices carry the
same entry on the diagonals, whereas Hankel matrices carry the same
entry on the anti-diagonals, for instance

(4.12)

T(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&a_{-1}&a_{-2}&\dots\\
a_{1}&a_{0}&a_{-1}&\dots\\
a_{2}&a_{1}&a_{0}&\dots\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad H(a)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&a_{3}&\dots\\
a_{2}&a_{3}&\dots&\dots\\
a_{3}&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
H(\chi_{n})=0
for
n\leqslant 0
, and that
H(\chi_{n})
has rank
n
for
n\geqslant 1
.

The quantization procedures
p\mapsto\mathrm{Op}(p)
and
p\mapsto P_{K}(p)
are clearly linear.
Composition of such operators is however more complicated. Given
a,b\in W
we have that

(4.13)

\mathrm{Op}(ab)=\mathrm{Op}(a)\mathrm{Op}(b).

However, the composition of two Toeplitz matrices is in general not a Toeplitz matrix but only a Toeplitz
matrix modulo two products of Hankel matrices, see for instance
[
15
, Proposition 3.10]
.
Given
a,b\in W
we have,
with
\Pi_{N}=\mathbf{1}_{[0,N-1]}
and
\widetilde{\Pi}_{N}=\Pi_{N}H(\chi_{N})\Pi_{N}
, that

(4.14)

T_{N}(ab)=T_{N}(a)T_{N}(b)+\Pi_{N}H(a)H(\widetilde{b})\Pi_{N}+\widetilde{\Pi}_{N}H(\widetilde{a})H(b)\widetilde{\Pi}_{N}.

In this paper we mainly consider the operator

(4.15)

\mathrm{Op}(p)=\sum_{-N_{-}}^{N_{+}}a_{j}\tau^{j},\quad a_{-N_{-}},a_{-N_{-}+1},\dots,a_{N_{+}}\in\mathds{C},\penalty\ a_{\pm N_{\pm}}\neq 0

acting on
\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z})
or, more generally, on functions
\psi:\mathds{Z}\to\mathds{C}
, whose coefficients
satisfy Assumption
1.4
, and with symbol
p
given by

(4.16)

\mathds{C}\backslash\{0\}\ni\zeta\mapsto p(\zeta)=\sum_{-N_{-}}^{N_{+}}a_{j}\zeta^{-j}.

4.2.
Circulant matrices

In this section we discuss circulant matrices, which are
close relatives
of Toeplitz matrices that play an important role in our analysis.

For
N\geqslant 1
and
p\in W
we consider
\mathrm{Op}(p)
(
4.7
) acting on
\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z})
which
we identify with the space of
N
-periodic functions on
\mathds{Z}
. To distinguish this case from
the other operators considered in this paper, we write

(4.17)

P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathrm{Op}(p):\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z})\to\ell^{2}(\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}).

For
\nu\in\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}

we get that

(P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}u)(\nu)=\sum_{n\in\mathds{Z}}u(\nu-n)=\sum_{\mu\in\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}}p_{\nu-\mu+kN}\,u(\mu),

so the matrix elements of
P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}
are given by

(4.18)

P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}(\nu,\mu)=\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}}p_{\nu-\mu+kN},\quad\mu,\nu\in\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}.

Identifying
[0,N-1]\simeq\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}
, we see by (
4.9
) that for
p
as in (
4.16
),

(4.19)

P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}(\nu,\mu)-P_{N}(\nu,\mu)=p_{\nu-\mu+N}+p_{\nu-\mu-N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}B(\nu,\mu),\quad\mu,\nu\in[0,N-1],

where
B
is of rank
N_{+}+N_{-}
. Using the discrete Fourier transform, with

\mathcal{F}_{N}=N^{-1/2}(\exp(-2\pi inm/N))_{0\leqslant n,m\leqslant N-1}

we may
represent
P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}
by

(4.20)

P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}=\mathcal{F}_{N}^{*}\,\mathrm{diag}(p(e^{2\pi il/N});0\leqslant l\leqslant N-1)\,\mathcal{F}_{N},

which immediately shows that the spectrum of
P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}}
is
given by
\sigma(P_{\mathds{Z}/N\mathds{Z}})=\{p(e^{2\pi il/N});0\leqslant l\leqslant N-1)\}
.

4.3.
Roots of a Laurent polynomial

In this section we discuss the roots
of the Laurent polynomial

(4.21)

p(\zeta)=\sum_{-\mathfrak{N}_{-}}^{\mathfrak{N}_{+}}a_{j}\zeta^{-j},\quad-\mathfrak{N}_{-}\leqslant\mathfrak{N}_{+},\quad a_{-\mathfrak{N}_{-}},a_{-\mathfrak{N}_{-}+1},\dots,a_{\mathfrak{N}_{+}}\in\mathds{C},\quad a_{\pm\mathfrak{N}_{\pm}}\neq 0.

It will be convenient to extend the symbol
p
to a holomorphic map

\widetilde{p}:\widehat{\mathds{C}}\to\widehat{\mathds{C}}

on the extended complex plane
\widehat{\mathds{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathds{C}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\infty\}
. Here
\widehat{\mathds{C}}
is
a complex manifold, equipped with the topology given by
U\in\widehat{\mathds{C}}
open if either
U\subset\mathds{C}
open
or
\widehat{\mathds{C}}\backslash U\subset\mathds{C}
is compact, and with the equivalence class of holomorphic
atlases represented by
\{\mathrm{id}:\mathds{C}\to\mathds{C},\penalty\ \phi:\widehat{\mathds{C}}\backslash\{0\}\to\mathds{C}\}
where

\phi(\infty)=0
and
\phi(z)=1/z
for
z\notin\{0,\infty\}
. In what follows we will drop the tilde notation and
denote by
p
also the extension.

We exclude the case of constant Laurent polynomials, i.e. we assume that

(4.22)

\mathfrak{N}_{\pm}>0\text{ when }\mathfrak{N}_{\mp}=0.

We say that
\zeta\in\mathds{C}\,\dot{\cup}\{\infty\}
is a root of (
4.21
) when

(4.23)

p(\zeta)=\sum_{-\mathfrak{N}_{-}}^{\mathfrak{N}_{+}}a_{j}\zeta^{-j}=0,

and, by using the change of coordinates
\zeta=1/\omega
, we have that
\zeta=\infty

is a root if
\omega=0
is a root of
p(1/\omega)
. We keep in mind that in the sequel we will
be interested in symbols whose coefficients
a_{j}
may depend on a spectral parameter
z\in\mathds{C}
.
For instance the coefficient of order zero of the symbol
p(\zeta)-z
is
a_{0}-z
, and all the other
coefficients remain independent of
z
.

Lemma 4.1
.

The Laurent polynomial (
4.21
), assuming (
4.22
), has

\mathfrak{N}=\mathfrak{N}_{+}\lor 0+\mathfrak{N}_{-}\lor 0
roots
in
\mathds{C}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\infty\}
.
Assume that
0\notin p(S^{1})
, and let

(4.24)

\zeta_{1}^{+},\dots,\zeta_{m_{+}}^{+}\text{ denote the roots in }D(0,1),

and

(4.25)

\zeta_{1}^{-},\dots,\zeta_{m_{-}}^{-}\text{ denote the roots in }(\mathds{C}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\infty\})\backslash\overline{D(0,1)},

where
m_{+}+m_{-}=\mathfrak{N}
(working with the convention that when
m_{+}=0
resp.
m_{-}=0
,
we have no roots (
4.24
) resp. (
4.25
)).
We can distinguish the following three cases:

Case 1
If
\mathfrak{N}_{\pm}\geqslant 0
, then we have that

(4.26)

0<|\zeta_{1}^{+}|\leqslant\cdots\leqslant|\zeta_{m_{+}}^{+}|<1<|\zeta_{1}^{-}|\leqslant\cdots\leqslant|\zeta_{m_{-}}^{-}|<+\infty

Case 2
When
\mathfrak{N}_{+}<0
, then we have
that
\infty
is not a root but
0
is a root of (
4.23
) of multiplicity

m_{0}\in[1\lor|\mathfrak{N}_{+}|,|\mathfrak{N}_{-}|]
. In this case, we have that

(4.27)

0=|\zeta_{1}^{+}|=\cdots=|\zeta_{m_{0}}^{+}|<|\zeta_{m_{0}+1}^{+}|\leqslant\cdots\leqslant|\zeta_{m_{+}}^{+}|<1<|\zeta_{1}^{-}|\leqslant\cdots\leqslant|\zeta_{m_{-}}^{-}|<+\infty.

Case 3
When
\mathfrak{N}_{-}<0
, then
0
is no root, but
\infty
is a root of multiplicity

m_{\infty}\in[1\lor|\mathfrak{N}_{-}|,|\mathfrak{N}_{+}|],
and we may order the roots as

(4.28)

0<|\zeta_{1}^{+}|\leqslant\cdots\leqslant|\zeta_{m_{+}}^{+}|<1<|\zeta_{1}^{-}|\leqslant\cdots\leqslant|\zeta^{-}_{m_{-}-m_{\infty}}|<+\infty=|\zeta_{m_{-}-m_{\infty}+1}^{-}|=\dots=|\zeta_{m_{-}}^{-}|.

Proof.

1. If
\mathfrak{N}_{\pm}\geqslant 0
, we see that neither
0
nor
\infty
can be a root of (
4.23
),
which therefore has the same roots as

(4.29)

\zeta^{\mathfrak{N}_{+}}p(\zeta)=\sum_{0}^{\mathfrak{N}_{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{-}}a_{\mathfrak{N}_{+}-j}\zeta^{j}=0,

a polynomial of degree
\mathfrak{N}_{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{-}
. We order
its
\mathfrak{N}_{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{-}
roots, counted with
their multiplicities, as in (
4.24
), (
4.25
), with

m_{+}+m_{-}=\mathfrak{N}_{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{-}
, and we conclude (
4.26
).

2. When
\mathfrak{N}_{+}<0
then (
4.23
) is given by

(4.30)

p(\zeta)=\sum_{|\mathfrak{N}_{+}|}^{\mathfrak{N}_{-}}a_{-j}\zeta^{j}=0,

a polynomial of degree
\mathfrak{N}_{-}
and so we have
\mathfrak{N}_{-}
roots in
\mathds{C}
and

\infty
cannot be a root. It follows from (
4.30
) that
0
is a root with multiplicity

(4.31)

m_{0}=\min\{j\in\{|\mathfrak{N}_{+}|,\dots,\mathfrak{N}_{-}\};a_{j}-z\delta_{j,0}\neq 0\},

where we recall the Dirac notation from Section
1.6
.
Notice that

\max\{1,|\mathfrak{N}_{+}|\}\leqslant m_{0}\leqslant|\mathfrak{N}_{-}|
.
In this case, we denote the roots as in (
4.24
), (
4.25
) and (
4.27
).

3. If
\mathfrak{N}_{-}<0
then (
4.23
) is given by

(4.32)

p(\zeta)=\sum_{|\mathfrak{N}_{-}|}^{\mathfrak{N}_{+}}a_{j}\zeta^{-j}=0.

So
0
is not a root, however,
\infty
maybe be one. Performing the change of variables

\omega=1/\zeta
we see that
p(1/\omega)
satisfies the
assumptions of
Step 2
with the roles
\mathfrak{N}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{N}_{+}
exchanged and roots
\omega_{j}^{\pm}=1/\zeta_{j}^{\pm}
. Hence, we conclude (
4.28
) when Case 3 holds.
∎

4.4.
Kernels of
P_{[0,\infty[}-z
and
P_{]-\infty,0]}-z

In this section we discuss how to construct the elements of the kernels of

P_{[0,\infty[}-z
and
P_{]-\infty,0]}-z
, see (
4.8
), where
p
is as in (
4.16
),
(
4.15
),
z\in\mathds{C}\backslash p(S^{1})
.

Recall that the index of a Fredholm operator
A
on a Hilbert space is given by

\mathrm{Ind}(A)=\dim\mathcal{N}(A)-\dim\mathcal{N}(A^{*}).

Here
\mathcal{N}(A)
denotes the kernel of
A
, and note that
the kernel of
A^{*}
is isomorphic to the cokernel of
A
.
We know from

[
15
, Theorem 1.9]
,
[
65
]
that
P_{K}-z
, for
K=]-\infty,0]
and
[0,\infty[
,
are Fredholm operators, and that the winding
number
\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)
of the curve
p(S^{1})
around
z
is related to the
Fredholm index of
P_{K}-z
as follows:

(4.33)

\mathrm{Ind}(P_{[0,\infty[}-z)=\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z),\quad\mathrm{Ind}(P_{]-\infty,0]}-z)=-\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z).

Using Lemma
4.1
one can express the winding number of
p(S^{1})
around
z
by

(4.34)

\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{S^{1}}\frac{d}{d\eta}\log(p(\eta)-z)d\eta=m_{+}-(N_{+}\lor 0)=(N_{-}\lor 0)-m_{-}.

Remark 4.2
.

If the symbol
p-z
satisfies Case
2
of Lemma
4.1
, then
\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)=m_{+}>0
, and if it satisfies Case
3
, then
\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)=-m_{-}<0
.

When comparing with the literature, for instance
[
15
]
, then the right hand sides of the equations in (
4.34
) and (
4.33
) have the opposite sign. This discrepancy comes from our choice
to write the Fourier series (
4.1
) with a minus instead of a plus sign in the exponential.

Our aim is now to give explicit expressions for the kernel vectors
of
P_{[0,\infty[}-z
and
P_{]-\infty,0]}-z
depending on the roots of
the symbol
p-z
. We will only treat the case when the symbol satisfies
the assumptions of Case 1 of Lemma
4.1
since the other
two cases are non-generic. Furthermore, we will only work in
the case where all roots of
p-z
are simple. We will see that under
these assumptions the kernel vectors will be given by exponential
solutions as in (
4.37
) below, corresponding to the roots in

\mathds{C}\backslash\{0\}
.

4.4.1.
Exponential solutions

We begin with a slightly more general discussion on exponential solutions.
Let
\zeta\in\mathds{C}\backslash\{0\}
be a root of (
4.23
) with multiplicity
\mathrm{mult(\zeta)}
,
then the exponential functions

(4.35)

\mathds{Z}\ni\nu\mapsto f_{\zeta,k}(\nu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\nu^{k}(\zeta^{\nu}),\ 0\leqslant k\leqslant\mathrm{mult\,}(\zeta)-1

are solutions to

(4.36)

\mathrm{Op}(p)f_{\zeta,k}=0,\text{ on }\mathds{Z},

and these functions are linearly independent, by the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3

(
[
65
, Proposition 3.1]
)
.

Let
\zeta_{1},...,\zeta_{\mathfrak{N}}\in\mathds{C}\setminus\{0\}

be distinct numbers and let
1\leqslant m_{j}<\infty
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant\mathfrak{N}
.
The functions
f_{\zeta_{j},k}\,:\mathds{Z}\to\mathds{C}
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant\mathfrak{N}
,
0\leqslant k\leqslant m_{j}-1
are linearly independent. More precisely, if
K\subset\mathds{Z}

is an interval with
\#K=m_{1}+m_{2}+...+m_{\mathfrak{N}}
, then
{{f_{\zeta_{j},k}}_{|}}_{K}
form a basis in
\ell^{2}(K)
.

This result together with Lemma
4.1
immediately yield the
following result.

Proposition 4.4

(
[
65
, Proposition 3.7]
)
.

Let
p
be as in (
4.21
), (
4.22
) with
\mathfrak{N}_{\pm}\geqslant 0
. Suppose
that
0\notin p(S^{1})
, that all roots of
p(\zeta)
are simple and ordered as in
(
4.26
).

Then, the space of exponential solutions to
\mathrm{Op}(p)u=0
is of dimension

m_{+}+m_{-}
, and the general solution is of the form

(4.37)

u(\nu)=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{+}}a_{j}^{+}(\zeta_{j}^{+})^{\nu}+\sum_{j=1}^{m_{-}}a_{j}^{-}(\zeta_{j}^{-})^{\nu},\quad a^{\pm}_{j}\in\mathds{C}.

The subspace of solutions decaying as
\nu\to+\infty
is given by

(4.38)

a_{j}^{-}=0,\quad\text{for }\penalty\ 0\leqslant j\leqslant m_{-}

and the subspace of solutions decaying as
\nu\to-\infty
is given by

(4.39)

a_{j}^{+}=0,\quad\text{for }1\leqslant j\leqslant m_{+}

Proof.

The statement on the dimension and (
4.37
) are an immediate consequence of
Lemma
4.1
and Proposition
4.3
. The last statement is a consequence
of the fact that
|\zeta_{j}^{+}|<1
and
|\zeta_{j}^{-}|>1
.
∎

4.4.2.
Eigenvectors in Case 1

We recall
[
65
, Proposition 3.6]
in a slightly modified form to fit our somewhat different
notation.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
p
be as in (
4.21
), (
4.22
) with
\mathfrak{N}_{\pm}\geqslant 0
.
Let
K\subset\mathds{Z}
be an interval of length
\leqslant\mathfrak{N}_{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{-}
.
Any function
u:\penalty\ K\to\mathds{C}
can be extended to a solution
\widetilde{u}:\penalty\ \mathds{Z}\to\mathds{C}

to
\mathrm{Op}(p)\widetilde{u}=0
. The space of such extensions is affine of
dimension
\mathfrak{N}_{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{-}-\#K
. In particular the extension is unique when
\mathfrak{N}_{+}+\mathfrak{N}_{-}=\#K
.

Let
p
be as in (
4.15
), (
4.16
) and let
z\in\mathds{C}\backslash p(S^{1})
. We assume that the
symbol
p(\zeta)-z
satisfies Case 1 of Lemma
4.1

(with
p(\zeta)-z
replacing
p(\zeta)
in (
4.21
)).
This translates to

(4.40)

\begin{split}&\bullet\quad N_{\pm}>0,\\
&\bullet\quad\text{or }z\neq a_{0}\text{ when }N_{-}=0,\text{ or }z\neq 0\text{ when }N_{-}<0,\\
&\bullet\quad\text{or }z\neq a_{0}\text{ when }N_{+}=0,\text{ or }z\neq 0\text{ when }N_{+}<0.\end{split}

By Lemma
4.1
, we have
m_{+}+m_{-}=N_{+}\lor 0+N_{-}\lor 0
roots of
p(\zeta)-z
.

We now turn to the operators
P_{[0,\infty[}-z
and
P_{]-\infty,0]}-z
. The two cases
are similar, so we focus on the first one and identify
[0,\infty[\simeq\mathds{N}
whenever
convenient. The following discussion is a modified version of the one in
[
65
]
,
presented here for the reader’s convenience.

Let
u\in\ell^{2}(\mathds{N})
be so that
(P_{[0,\infty[}-z)u=0
on
\mathds{N}
. When
N_{+}>0
we put

(4.41)

u(-{N_{+}})=\dots=u(-1)=0,

and when
N_{+}\leqslant 0
, we do not put (
4.41
). Then we see that, for
\nu=0,1,\dots
,

(4.42)

\left(\sum_{-N_{-}}^{N_{+}}a_{j}\tau^{j}-z\right)u(\nu)=0.

Continuing, we then know how to extend
u\!\upharpoonright_{[-(N_{+}\lor 0),\infty[}
to a function

\widetilde{u}:\mathds{Z}\to\mathds{C}
satisfying
(\mathrm{Op}(p)-z)\widetilde{u}=0
on
\mathds{Z}
, by solving
(
4.42
) with
u
replaced by
\widetilde{u}
and for
\nu=-1,-2,\dots
. More precisely,
the equation for
\nu=-1
defines uniquely
\widetilde{u}(-N_{+}\lor 0-1)
and the
next one gives
\widetilde{u}(-N_{+}\lor 0-2)
. Continuing in this way we get a solution

\widetilde{u}
of
(\mathrm{Op}(p)-z)\widetilde{u}=0
on
\mathds{Z}
. Since we are in Case 1, Proposition

4.4
implies that
\widetilde{u}
is
of the form (
4.37
).

Since
u\in\ell^{2}(\mathds{N})
, it follows by (
4.38
) that

(4.43)

\widetilde{u}(\nu)=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{+}}a_{j}^{+}(\zeta_{j}^{+})^{\nu}.

When
N_{+}>0
we have by construction that

\widetilde{u}(\nu)=u(\nu)=0
for
\nu\in[-N_{+},-1]
, which implies
that the coefficients
a_{j}^{+}
in (
4.43
) are determined by

(4.44)

\begin{split}&0=\mathfrak{A}\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{+}\\
\vdots\\
a_{m_{+}}^{+}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathfrak{A}=(\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{+},\dots,\mathfrak{A}_{m_{+}}^{+})\in\mathds{C}^{N_{+}\times m_{+}},\\
&\mathfrak{A}_{j}^{+}=((\zeta_{j}^{+})^{\nu})_{-N_{+}\leqslant\nu\leqslant-1},\quad\text{for }j=1,\dots,m_{+}.\end{split}

Notice that
\mathfrak{A}
is a rectangular matrix of size
N_{+}\times m_{+}
. Recall from Proposition
4.3

that the exponential functions (
4.35
) restricted to an interval
K\Subset\mathds{Z}
of
length
|K|=m_{+}
are linearly independent. Thus, the linear system in the first line of (
4.44
)
has
m_{+}-N_{+}
linearly independent solutions if
m_{+}-N_{+}\geqslant 0
, and none when
m_{+}-N_{+}<0
.
This implies that
\dim\mathcal{N}(P_{[0,+\infty[}-z)=(m_{+}-N_{+})\lor 0
.

Similarly, when
N_{+}\leqslant 0
, we have
no constraints on the coefficients in (
4.43
), which yields

m_{+}\lor 0
linearly independent solutions, so
\dim\mathcal{N}(P_{[0,+\infty[}-z)=m_{+}\lor 0
. Thus

(4.45)

\dim\mathcal{N}(P_{[0,+\infty[}-z)=(m_{+}-N_{+}\lor 0)\lor 0.

Similarly one also obtains the corresponding statements for the kernel of
(P_{]-\infty,0]}-z)
with the
N_{+}
,
m_{+}
replaced by
N_{-}
,
m_{-}
. Summing up what we have proven so far, we get in view of (
4.33
), (
4.34
), see
[
65
]
for similar statements:

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
p
be as in (
4.15
), (
4.16
). Let
z\in\mathds{C}\backslash p(S^{1})
. Assume
that the assumptions of Case 1 in Lemma
4.1
hold for
p(\zeta)-z
, see also
(
4.40
). Then

\dim\mathcal{N}\left(P_{[0,+\infty[}-z\right)=\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)\lor 0

and
u\in\mathcal{N}\left(P_{[0,+\infty[}-z\right)
if and only if
u\in\ell^{2}([0,\infty[)
is such that
\widetilde{u}
defined above is of the form

(4.46)

\widetilde{u}(\nu)=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{+}}a_{j}^{+}(\zeta_{j}^{+})^{\nu},\quad a^{+}_{j}\in\mathds{C},

with
a^{+}_{j}
additionally satisfying (
4.44
) when
N_{+}>0
. Furthermore,

\dim\mathcal{N}\left(P_{]-\infty,0]}-z\right)=(-\mathrm{ind}_{p(S^{1}}(z))\lor 0.

and
u\in\mathcal{N}\left(P_{]-\infty,0]}-z\right)
if and only if
u\in\ell^{2}(]-\infty,0]
is of the form

(4.47)

\widetilde{u}(\nu)=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{-}}a_{j}^{-}(\zeta_{j}^{-})^{\nu},\quad a^{-}_{j}\in\mathds{C},

with
a^{-}_{j}
additionally satisfying the analog of (
4.44
) (with
m_{+},N_{+},\zeta_{j}^{+}

replaced by
m_{-},N_{-},\zeta_{j}^{-}
and the corresponding vectors
\mathfrak{A}_{j}^{-}
indexed
over
1\leqslant\nu\leqslant N_{-}
), when
N_{-}>0
.

5.
Separation of the eigenvalues from the spectral curve

As discussed in the introduction, in our setup all but
o(N)
of the
eigenvalues of
P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}
lie in a small neighborhood of
p(S^{1})
.
In this section we obtain complementary,
precise estimates on the location of (all) the eigenvalues for the finitely banded case:
we will show that if the perturbation is small
in the sense that
\gamma>1
, then the eigenvalues avoid certain
N
-dependent regions, referred to as
forbidden tubes
(see Definition
5.1
below), around the spectral curve.
These tubes are determined in terms of the roots of the Laurent polynomial
p_{z}(\cdot)=0
, where

(5.1)

p_{z}(\zeta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}p(\zeta)-z=\sum_{-N_{-}}^{N_{+}}a_{j}\zeta^{-j}-z=0,

with
\zeta\in\mathds{C}\dot{\cup}\{\infty\}
and
z\in\mathds{C}
.
As before, we will exclude the trivial case
N_{\pm}=0
. Furthermore, if
N_{+}<0
then we
modify
p
by setting
a_{j}=0
for
j=N_{+}+1,\ldots,0
. This allows us, without loss of generality, to assume that
N_{+}\geqslant 0
.

To state the main result of this section we need to
introduce some notation. With the goal of being consistent
with the notation of
[
9
]
,
we write
\{\eta_{j}(z)\}_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}
for
the negative of the roots of
p_{z}(\cdot)=0

arranged in a non-increasing order of their moduli.
For
z\notin p(S^{1})
we let
m_{+}=m_{+}(z)
denote
the number of roots of
p_{z}(\cdot)
that are in
{D(0,1)}
,
while the number of roots in

(\mathds{C}\cup\{\infty\})\setminus\overline{D(0,1)}
is denoted

m_{-}=m_{-}(z)
. Therefore,

\widetilde{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}m_{+}+m_{-}=N_{+}+\max\{N_{-},0\},

and

|\eta_{1}(z)|\geqslant|\eta_{2}(z)|\geqslant\cdots\geqslant|\eta_{m_{-}}(z)|>1>|\eta_{m_{-}+1}(z)|\geqslant\cdots\geqslant|\eta_{\widetilde{m}}(z)|.

Compared with the previous notation, see e.g. Lemma
4.1
,
we have that
\eta_{i}=-\zeta^{-}_{m_{-}-i+1}
for
i=1,\ldots,m_{-}
and

\eta_{m_{-}+i}=-\zeta^{+}_{m_{+}-i+1}
for
i=1,\ldots,m_{+}
.
Recall the notation
{\sf g}(\cdot)
from (
1.6
). The parameter
{\sf g}(\cdot)
will determine the width of forbidden tubes,
see Remark
5.6
.

The definition of the forbidden
tubes also involves
the winding number of
p(S^{1})
. For
d\in\mathds{Z}
we set

(5.2)

\mathcal{S}_{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\{z\in\mathds{C}\setminus p(S^{1}):m_{+}(z)-N_{+}=d\}.

Note that for
z\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
,
the winding number
{\rm ind}_{p(S^{1})}(z)
equals
d
. Also observe that on
\mathcal{S}_{d}
the functions
z\mapsto m_{\pm}(z)
are constants and those common values are determined by
d
and
N_{\pm}
.

Recall that

{\rm cl}(A)
denotes the closure of a set
A\subset\mathds{C}
.
We can now define the forbidden tubes.

Definition 5.1

(Forbidden tubes)
.

Let
p(\cdot)
be a Laurent polynomial.
For
z\in\mathds{C}
, let
p_{z}(\cdot)
be as in (
5.1
), and write

{\sf g}(p)={\sf g}_{0}\in\mathds{Z}
. Fix
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
\gamma^{\prime}>1
, and
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}>0
such that
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}<\varepsilon_{0}
.

•

For
d\in[0,\widetilde{m}]\cap\mathds{Z}
we set
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(1)}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(2)}
, where for
d>0

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\rm cl}\Big({\Big\{z\in{\mathcal{S}_{d}}:\max\left\{{|\eta_{m_{-}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}(z)|,|\eta_{m_{-}}(z)|^{-1}}\right\}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon_{0},}\\
{1-\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}}\leqslant{|\eta_{m_{-}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}+1}(z)|}<1\Big\}\Big),

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{0,(1)}=\emptyset
, and

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(2)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\Big\{z\in{\mathcal{S}_{d}}:\max\left\{|\eta_{m_{-}+1}(z)|,|\eta_{m_{-}-{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|^{-1}\right\}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon_{0},\\
1-\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}-{\sf g}_{0}+1}(z)|^{-1}\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}}(z)|^{-1}<1\Big\}.

•

For
d\in[-\widetilde{m},0)\cap\mathds{Z}
we set
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(1)}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(2)}
, where

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\rm cl}\Big(\Big\{z\in{\mathcal{S}_{d}}:\max\left\{|\eta_{m_{-}+1}(z)|,|\eta_{m_{-}-{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|^{-1}\right\}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon_{0},\\
1-\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}-{\sf g}_{0}+1}(z)|^{-1}\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}}(z)|^{-1}<1\Big\},

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(2)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\Big\{z\in{\mathcal{S}_{d}}:\max\left\{|\eta_{m_{-}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}(z)|,|\eta_{m_{-}}(z)|^{-1}\right\}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon_{0},\\
1-\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}+1}(z)|<1\Big\}.

For all
d
,
set

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(s)}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{T}^{d,(s)}_{(\gamma^{\prime}-1)\log N/N,\varepsilon_{0}},s\in\{1,2\},

and write

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(1)}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(2)}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}.

Note that
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}
is a (union of)
tubes of vanishing width, whereas the width of

\mathcal{T}^{d}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}
is small but fixed.
When the choices of the parameters
\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}
are clear from the context we will suppress the dependence of the tubes on these parameters, and
write
\mathcal{T}^{d}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d}
,
\mathcal{T}^{d,(s)}
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(s)}
, for
s\in\{1,2\}
.

Remark 5.2
.

The map
z\mapsto\{\eta_{j}(z)\}_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}
is continuous in the symmetric product topology
[
74
, Appendix 5, Theorem 4A]
. Therefore, the map
z\mapsto(|\eta_{1}(z)|,|\eta_{2}(z)|,\ldots,|\eta_{\widetilde{m}}(z)|)
is continuous as well. This implies in particular that for
d>0
and any
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}_{\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}}
, one has that

\max\left\{{|\eta_{m_{-}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}(z)|,|\eta_{m_{-}}(z)|^{-1}}\right\}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon_{0},\quad\text{ and }\quad{1-\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}}\leqslant{|\eta_{m_{-}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|\leqslant|\eta_{m_{-}+1}(z)|}\leqslant 1,

where
m_{\pm}
are determined by
d
and
N_{\pm}
. When
d<0
a similar assertion holds for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}_{\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}}
.

Remark 5.3
.

While proving the results in this section, and Sections
6
and
7
we will need to consider
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}
such that
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}/\varepsilon_{0}
is a small fraction depending only on the degree of the Laurent polynomial
p(\cdot)
. The specific choices will be spelled out during the proofs.

We illustrate Definition
5.1
in some examples. Consider
first the simplest setup when the Toeplitz matrix
P_{N}
is the
Jordan block
J_{N}
given by

(J_{N})_{i,j}={\bf 1}_{j=i+1}
for
i,j\in[N]
. In this case,
\mathcal{T}^{1,(1)}=\overline{D(0,1)}\setminus D(0,1-\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})
, whereas

\mathcal{T}^{0,(2)}
is
\overline{D(0,1+\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})}\setminus\overline{D(0,1)}
.
The rest of the tubes are empty.
When the spectral curve
p(S^{1})
is a Lima0con, i.e.
P_{N}=J_{N}+J_{N}^{2}
has
symbol

p(\zeta)=\zeta+\zeta^{2}
,
the union of the tubes is a region around the entire spectral curve,
except for a small neighborhood, determined by
\varepsilon_{0}
,
around the point on
p(S^{1})
where the curve intersects itself.
See Figure
3
.

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{1,(1)}
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{1,(2)}
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{2,(1)}
\mathcal{S}_{0}
\mathcal{S}_{1}
\mathcal{S}_{2}

Figure 3.
The different tubes for the Lima0con, where

p(\zeta)=\zeta+\zeta^{2}
,
N_{+}=0
, and
d=0,1,2
according to whether
z
is outside the curve, inside the
larger loop, or inside the inner loop. The shaded disc
is
\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}
, for some
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
(recall Definition
1.3
).
Theorem
5.4
precludes the appearance of
eigenvalues
of small
random additive perturbations of
P_{N}=J_{N}+J_{N}^{2}
in a region away of the shaded area that includes

\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{1,(1)}
,
while Theorem
5.7
handles
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{1,(2)}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{2,(1)}
.

Below is the first main result of this section. Hereafter, for any
z\in\mathds{C}
we use the shorthand
P_{z}^{\delta}:=P_{N,\gamma}^{Q}-zI_{N}
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Fix
\gamma>1
. Set
\delta=N^{-\gamma}
. Let
p(\cdot)
be a Laurent polynomial and
P_{N}
be the
N\times N
Toeplitz matrix with symbol
p(\cdot)
, and the entries of the noise matrix
Q
satisfies Assumption
1.1
. Then, for any
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
there exists some constant
\widehat{c}_{\gamma,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}>0
, depending on
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}
and
\gamma
, such that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\left(\exists z\in\mathcal{S}_{0}^{\widehat{c}_{\gamma,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}\log N/N}\setminus(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}):\det(P_{z}^{\delta})=0\right)=0.

Remark 5.5
.

The proof of Theorem
5.4
yields that one can take

(5.3)

\widehat{c}_{\gamma,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}=\frac{\gamma^{\prime}-1}{\sup_{(p(S^{1}))^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}}\max_{j\in[\widetilde{m}]}|\frac{d}{dz}\eta_{j}(z)|},

for any
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
1<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
. By the Implicit Function Theorem we have that

(5.4)

\sup_{\mathds{D}}\max_{j\in[\widetilde{m}]}\left|\frac{d}{dz}\eta_{j}(z)\right|=O(1),

for any bounded domain
\mathds{D}
such that
\mathrm{dist\,}(\mathds{D},\mathcal{B}_{2})>0
.
Therefore, if we impose the additional assumption
that
\mathrm{dist\,}(p(S^{1}),\mathcal{B}_{2})>0
,
then for
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
sufficiently small the
constant
\widehat{c}_{\gamma,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}
in Theorem
5.4
can be chosen to be free of
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}
.
In particular, in the simplest case when
P_{N}=J_{N}
, the
elementary Jordan block,
Theorem
5.4
holds with any
\widehat{c}_{\gamma,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}=\widehat{c}_{\gamma}<(\gamma-1)
.
We note that in that example
with
Q_{N}
a complex Ginibre matrix,
Davies and Hager
[
22
]
showed that for
\gamma\geqslant 7
, with high probability
