all eigenvalues are contained in
D(0,1-(\gamma-3){\log N}/{N})
.
Thus, Theorem
5.4
provides a sharper
result for general finitely banded Toeplitz matrices, under a fairly generic assumption on
Q_{N}
.

Remark 5.6
.

Let
{\sf g}_{0}\in\mathds{N}
and consider the symbol

p(\zeta)=\zeta^{{\sf g}_{0}}
. Then the empirical measures of the eigenvalues of
random perturbations of the corresponding Toeplitz matrices
converge to the uniform measure on
S^{1}
, see

[
7
,
8
,
65
]
. However, using (
5.3
),
one obtains from Theorem

5.4
that for
\gamma>1
, with high
probability the eigenvalues must be inside the disk of radius
1-{\sf g}_{0}\cdot{(\gamma^{\prime}-1)\log N}/{N}
, for any
\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
. The radius of the disk can be shown to tight upto
o(\log N/N)
.
Thus, although the parameter
{\sf g}(p)
plays no role in determining the limit of the empirical measure of the eigenvalues of randomly perturbed Toeplitz matrices, it indeed plays a role in determining the separation of the eigenvalues from the limiting spectral curve.

Theorem
5.4
shows that all eigenvalues of
P^{\delta}
must be at a distance at least
\widehat{c}_{\gamma,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}\log N/N
from the portion of
p(S^{1})
that intersects
{\rm cl}({S}_{0})
. To prove the localization of the eigenvectors we need to extend this picture to the regions with non-zero winding number. This is done in the result below.

Theorem 5.7
.

Fix
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
\gamma^{\prime}+1>\gamma>\gamma^{\prime}>1
and
d\in[-\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}]\cap\mathds{Z}
. Assume that
the noise matrix
Q
satisfies Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
. Then, for all
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{P}\left(\exists z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}:\det(P_{z}^{\delta})=0\right)=0.

While proving Theorems
5.4
and

5.7
, for convenience, we will avoid neighborhoods of bad points as defined in
Definition
1.3
. With additional efforts, it seems possible to extend our results also to neighborhoods of some
bad points, in particular those in
\mathcal{B}_{1}
. We do not purse that direction here.

Remark 5.8
.

Theorems
5.4
and

5.7
hold for
\gamma>1
.
We believe, and simulations suggest (see Figure
2
),
that this threshold for
\gamma
is sharp.
That is, for
\gamma<1

one may find eigenvalues in
{\rm cl}({\mathcal{S}}_{0})
with non-negligible
probability. Furthermore, the width of the tube, namely a constant multiple of

{\log N}/{N}
, is also sharp. See also Theorem
7.1
.

Remark 5.9
.

The papers

[
63
,
64
,
65
]
provide complementary estimates on the location of the eigenvalues around the curve
p(S^{1})
, showing
that for the finitely banded case,
\gamma
sufficiently large and any
\updelta_{0}>0
, all but
O(N^{2\updelta_{0}})
eigenvalues reside inside a tube around the spectral curve of width
O(N^{-\updelta_{0}})
, with high probability.

Remark 5.10
.

The proofs of Theorems
5.4
and
5.7
yield quantitative bounds on probabilities of the events considered. From the proof it follows that, for any
K_{\star}\in\mathds{N}
, the probability that there is no eigenvalue in
\mathcal{S}_{0}^{\widehat{c}_{\gamma,\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}\log N/N}\setminus(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}\cup\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}})
can be bounded by
O(N^{-K_{\star}})
. If additional assumptions on the moments of entries of
Q
are imposed then this bound can be further improved. For example, the assumption that the entries of
Q
have a uniform stretched exponential tail decay would imply a stretched exponential tail decay of the probability as well.
On the other hand, the proof of Theorem
5.7
yields that the probability that there are no eigenvalues of
P_{z}^{\delta}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{0}}
can be bounded by
O(N^{-(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\upeta/8})
, for
\upeta>0
as in Assumption

1.2
.

Remark 5.11
.

The determinant of a matrix and its transpose are the same. Furthermore, if
Q
satisfies Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
then so does
Q^{\sf T}
. Therefore, whenever
P_{N}
is a lower triangular Toeplitz matrix, to prove Theorems
5.4
and
5.7
we can work with
P_{N}^{\sf T}
. Thus,
while proving these two theorems, without loss of generality, we may and will assume that
N_{-}\geqslant 1
.

5.1.
Decomposition of the determinant

In this section
we decompose the determinant of
P_{z}^{\delta}
into the sum of
homogeneous polynomials
\{{\det}_{k}(z)\}_{k=0}^{N}
in
the entries of the noise matrix
Q
, see
(
5.6
) and (
5.5
) below. We
then suitably preprocess
{\det}_{k}(z)

so that various bounds on those terms
can be derived,
leading to a proof of Theorems

5.4
and
5.7
.
At a high level, this decomposition and some of the
preprocessing steps are as in

[
8
,
9
]
, where these were used
to study the empirical measure of the eigenvalues
and the process of
outlier eigenvalues, respectively. However, in
[
8
,
9
]

it was sufficient to derive bounds
when
z
avoids an
N
-independent

region around the spectral curve
p(S^{1})
. Here, we need to consider
an
N
-dependent
region around it, and this requires
better bounds and a more sophisticated analysis
than in
[
8
,
9
]
, especially in the proof of Theorem

5.7
.

For
k\in[N]
we set

(5.5)

{\det}_{k}(z)={\det}_{k,N}(z):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{X})\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{Y})}\det(P_{z}[{X}^{c};{Y}^{c}])N^{-\gamma k}\det(Q[X;Y]),

where for a matrix
A
the notation

A[X;Y]
denotes the sub-matrix of
A
induced by the rows in
X
and columns in
Y
,
{X}^{c}:=[N]\setminus X
,
{Y}^{c}:=[N]\setminus Y
, and for
Z\in\{X,Y\}
,
\sigma_{Z}
is the permutation on
[N]
which places all the elements of
Z
before all the elements of
{Z}^{c}
, but preserves the order of the elements within the two sets. Additionally denote

{\det}_{0}(z):=\det(P_{z})
.
From
[
8
, Lemma A.1]
it follows that

(5.6)

\det(P_{z}+N^{-\gamma}Q)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}{\det}_{k}(z).

We next represent

\{{\det}_{k}(z)\}_{k=1}^{N}
as linear combinations of products of determinants of certain bidiagonal and upper triangular Toeplitz matrices with coefficients that are determinants of sub-matrices of
Q
.
If
P_{z}
is an upper triangular matrix, as
\{-\eta_{j}(z)\}_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}
are the roots of the equation
p_{z}(\cdot)=0
, it is immediate
to check that

(5.7)

P_{z}=a_{-N_{-}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}(J_{N}+\eta_{j}(z)I_{N}),

where we recall that

J_{N}
is the elementary Jordan block
given by
(J_{N})_{i,j}=\delta_{i+1,j}
for
i,j\in[N]
, using the
Dirac notation from Section
1.6
. Then the desired representation is a consequence of the Cauchy-Binet theorem.
For a general Toeplitz matrix, (
5.7
)
does not hold. However, as in
[
8
]
,

P_{z}=P_{N}(z)
can be viewed as a certain sub-matrix of an upper triangular finitely banded Toeplitz matrix with a slightly larger dimension, and
one can use a product form for the latter
coupled with the Cauchy-Binet theorem.
To use this idea efficiently we borrow the following definition from
[
8
]
. As already alluded to above, without loss of generality, we will assume that
N_{+}\geqslant 0
and
N_{-}\geqslant 1
.

Definition 5.12

(Toeplitz with a shifted symbol)
.

Let
P_{N}(p)
be a Toeplitz matrix with the finite symbol

p(\tau)=\sum_{j=-N_{-}}^{N_{+}}a_{j}\tau^{j},\quad N_{+}\geqslant 0,N_{-}\geqslant 1.

For
n>N_{+}+N_{-}
,
z\in\mathds{C}
and

\bar{N}_{+},\bar{N}_{-}\in\mathds{N}_{0}
such that
\bar{N}_{+}+\bar{N}_{-}=N_{+}+N_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\widetilde{N}
,
let
P_{n}(p,z;\bar{N}_{-})
denote the
n\times n
Toeplitz matrix with symbol

\widetilde{p}(\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=-N_{-}}^{N_{+}}a^{\prime}_{j}\tau^{j+(N_{-}-\bar{N}_{-})},

where
a^{\prime}_{j}:=a_{j}-z\delta_{j,0}
,
j=-N_{-},-N_{-}+1,\ldots,N_{+}
.

From Definition
5.12

it follows that

(5.8)

P_{\widehat{N}_{+}}(p,z;\widetilde{N})=\begin{bmatrix}a_{N_{+}}&\cdots&a_{0}-z&\cdots&a_{-N_{-}}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&a_{N_{+}}&&a_{0}-z&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&&\ddots&&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\ddots&&\ddots&&a_{-N_{-}}\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&\ddots&&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&&&\ddots&\ddots&&a_{0}-z\\
\vdots&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&0&a_{N_{+}}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\widehat{N}_{+}:=N+N_{+}.

Therefore,

P_{N}(p_{z})=P_{N}(p,z;N_{-})=P_{\widehat{N}_{+}}(p,z;\widetilde{N})[[N];[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N_{+}]].

Recalling
(
5.5
) and writing
S+\ell
for the
Minkowski sum of the sets
S
and
\{\ell\}
, we obtain that

(5.9)

\displaystyle{\det}_{k}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{X})\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{Y})}\mathfrak{D}(X,Y)\cdot N^{-\gamma k}\cdot\det(Q[X;Y]),

where

(5.10)

\mathfrak{D}(X,Y)=\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\det(P_{\widehat{N}_{+}}(p,z;\widetilde{N})[{X}^{c};{Y}^{c}+N_{+}]).

To derive upper
bounds on
\{{\det}_{k}(z)\}_{k=0}^{N}
we will use
Assumption
1.1
to compute high moments of the
former (and also use Markov’s inequality). By (
5.9
),
this requires the computation of high moments of

\mathfrak{D}(X,Y)
for any
X,Y\subset[N]
.
To this end, we first simplify the expression for
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y)
. Because

P_{\widehat{N}_{+}}(p,z;\widetilde{N})
is an upper triangular Toeplitz matrix and

\{\eta_{j}(z)\}_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}
are the negative of the roots of the
equation
p_{z}(\cdot)=0
, we obtain as in (
5.7
) that

(5.11)

\displaystyle P_{\widehat{N}_{+}}(p,z;\widetilde{N})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\widetilde{N}}(a_{N_{+}-\ell}-z\delta_{\ell,N_{+}})J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}^{\ell}=a_{-N_{-}}\prod_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}(J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{j}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}}).

We split the product in (
5.11
) into three blocks: in the first block we consider the product over the roots that are greater than one in moduli and separated from one, in the second block we will have product over the roots that are close to one in moduli, and in the third we consider the product over the rest.
To write this decomposition efficiently we set

(5.12)

\widehat{m}_{1}=\widehat{m}_{1}(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}m_{-}&\mbox{ for }z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)},\\
m_{-}-{\sf g}_{0}&\mbox{ for }z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)},\end{array}\right.

and introduce, recalling (
1.6
) and that
{\sf g}(p)={\sf g}_{0}
,

(5.13)

\mathcal{P}_{z}=\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{N}_{+}}(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\prod_{j=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{j}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}}).

(Note that if
{\sf g}_{0}=1
then

\mathcal{P}_{z}
is a bidiagonal matrix. The case
{\sf g}_{0}>1
, in which

\mathcal{P}_{z}
is an upper triangular finitely banded Toeplitz matrix with
bandwidth
{\sf g}_{0}
, requires a
special combinatorial
analysis contained in Section
5.3
below.)
We now obtain from (
5.11
) that

(5.14)

P_{\widehat{N}_{+}}(p,z;\widetilde{N})=a_{-N_{-}}\prod_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}(J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{j}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}})\cdot\mathcal{P}_{z}\cdot\prod_{j=\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}^{\widetilde{m}}(J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{j}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}}),

where, as is our convention, an empty product equals one.
Notice that in the special case
{\sf g}_{0}=1
,
the decompositions (
5.11
) and (
5.14
)
are identical.
Before going further let us explain the advantage of the decomposition (
5.14
) over (
5.11
).

When
z
avoids a
N
-independent

region around the spectral curve all the roots
of
p_{z}(\cdot)=0
are also bounded
away from the unit circle.
For such
z
’s using the decomposition (
5.11
) appropriate bounds on quantities analogous to
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)
were derived in
[
8
,
9
]
. Under the setup of Theorem
5.7
,
when
\gamma>{\sf g}_{0}
, suitable adaptations of the arguments
in
[
8
,
9
]
yield good
bounds on
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)
, and these adaptations fail when
\gamma\leqslant{\sf g}_{0}
.
At a high level this is due to the fact that the arguments in
[
8
,
9
]
, while applying the Cauchy-Binet theorem to obtain a bound on
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)
, bound each of the roots
\{-\eta_{j}(z)\}_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}
by their moduli. Since, for any
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
, there are
{\sf g}_{0}
many roots close to one in moduli, this requires

\gamma>{\sf g}_{0}
. To push the analysis to the case

{\sf g}_{0}\geqslant\gamma>1
, we will use that the roots of the symbol of the
Toeplitz matrix
\mathcal{P}_{z}
are
those of
p_{z}(\cdot)=0

that are close to the unit circle.
Instead of bounding each of these roots by their moduli,
obtaining a bound on minors of
\mathcal{P}_{z}
by combinatorial means yields
cancellations.
Therefore, (
5.14
) is more useful than
(
5.11
) in the proof of
Theorem
5.7
, when
1<\gamma\leqslant{\sf g}_{0}
.

We now turn to deriving a tractable representation of
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)
. Set

(5.15)

\widehat{m}_{2}=\widehat{m}_{2}(z):=\widetilde{m}-\widehat{m}_{1}(z)-{\sf g}_{0}.

Fix
k\in[N]
, and sets
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}
and
\{Y_{i^{\prime}}\}_{i^{\prime}=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}
such that
|X_{i}|=k+N_{+}
, for
i\in[\widehat{m}_{1}+1]
, and
|Y_{i^{\prime}}|=k+N_{+}
, for
i^{\prime}\in[\widehat{m}_{2}+1]
.
Write

(5.16)

X_{i}:=\left\{x_{i,1}<x_{i,2}<\cdots<x_{i,k+N_{+}}\right\},\quad\mathcal{X}_{k}:=(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}),

(5.17)

Y_{i^{\prime}}:=\left\{y_{i^{\prime},1}<y_{i^{\prime},2}<\cdots<y_{i^{\prime},k+N_{+}}\right\},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{Y}_{k}:=(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}).

Set

(5.18)

\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})=\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k},z):=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\det\left((J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{i}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}})[\check{X}_{i};\check{X}_{i+1}]\right)

and

(5.19)

\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k})=\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k},z):=\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\det\left((J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{i^{\prime}+\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}})[\check{Y}_{i^{\prime}};\check{Y}_{i^{\prime}+1}]\right),

where
\check{Z}:=[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus Z
for any
Z\subset[\widehat{N}_{+}]
. We emphasize the notational difference between
\check{Z}
and
Z^{c}
. The former will be used to write the complement of
Z
when viewed as a subset of
[\widehat{N}_{+}]
, whereas for the latter
Z
will be viewed as a subset of
[N]
.

Now using the Cauchy-Binet theorem and the representation (
5.14
) we find that

(5.20)

\mathfrak{D}(X,Y)=\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{Y}_{k}}a_{-N_{-}}^{N-k}\cdot\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})\cdot\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])\cdot\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k}),

where the sum above is taken over
(\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{Y}_{k})
such that

(5.21)

X_{1}=X\cup[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N]\qquad\text{ and }\qquad Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}].

Next using
[
8
, Lemma A.3]
we find that

(5.22)

\det((J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{i}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}})[\check{X}_{i};\check{X}_{i+1}])=\eta_{i}(z)^{x_{i+1,1}-1}\cdot\left(\prod_{\ell=2}^{k+N_{+}}\eta_{i}(z)^{x_{i+1,\ell}-x_{i,\ell}-1}\right)\\
\cdot\eta_{i}(z)^{\widehat{N}_{+}-x_{i,k+N_{+}}}\cdot{\bf 1}\left\{x_{i+1,\ell}\leqslant x_{i,\ell}<x_{i+1,\ell+1},\ell\in[k+N_{+}]\right\},

where we have set
x_{i+1,k+N_{+}+1}=\widehat{N}_{+}+1
for convenience. Thus, in light of (
5.22
), we may restrict the sum in (
5.20
) over
\mathcal{X}_{k}
belonging to

(5.23)

\widehat{L}_{k}^{(1)}:=\{\mathcal{X}_{k}:\,1\leqslant x_{i+1,1}\leqslant x_{i,1}<x_{i+1,2}\leqslant x_{i,2}<\cdots<x_{i+1,k+N_{+}}\leqslant x_{i,k+N_{+}}\leqslant\widehat{N}_{+}\}.

It will also be convenient to further partition the set of all
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\widehat{L}_{k}^{(1)}
so that
\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})
is constant
in each block of that partition.
To this end,
for any
{\bm{\ell}}:=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1})
with
0\leqslant\ell_{i}\leqslant\widehat{N}_{+}
for

i\in[\widehat{m}_{1}]
, and
k\in[N]
, we let

L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}:=\{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\widehat{L}_{k}^{(1)}:\\
\,x_{i+1,1}+\sum_{j=2}^{k+N_{+}}(x_{i+1,j}-x_{i,j-1})+(\widehat{N}_{+}-x_{i,k+N_{+}})=\ell_{i}+k+N_{+},\text{ for all }i=1,2,\ldots,\widehat{m}_{1}\}.

If
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
for some
{\bm{\ell}}
, by (
5.22
), we have that

(5.24)

\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\det\left((J_{\widehat{N}_{+}}+\eta_{i}(z)I_{\widehat{N}_{+}})[\check{X}_{i};\check{X}_{i+1}]\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\eta_{i}(z)^{\ell_{i}}.

Recall that for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
the roots
\{\eta_{i}(z)\}_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}
are greater than one in moduli and bounded away from one. Thus, for large values of
\{\ell_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}
the
rhs
of (
5.24
) will be exponentially (in
N
) large. It would be useful to factor
out this exponential factor. So, using the observation that

x_{i+1,1}+\sum_{j=2}^{k+N_{+}}(x_{i+1,j}-x_{i,j-1})+\sum_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}}(x_{i,j}-x_{i+1,j})+(\widehat{N}_{+}-x_{i,k+N_{+}})=\widehat{N}_{+},

we have the following equivalent representation of
L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
:

(5.25)

L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}=\Big\{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\widehat{L}_{k}:\,\sum_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}}(x_{i,j}-x_{i+1,j}+1)=\hat{\ell}_{i};\ i\in[\widehat{m}_{1}]\Big\},\;\mbox{\rm where}\;\hat{\ell}_{i}:=\widehat{N}_{+}-\ell_{i}.

Since
x_{i+1,j}\leqslant x_{i,j}
for any
i\in[\widehat{m}_{1}]
, and
j\in[k+N_{+}]
, we further note from (
5.25
) that

(5.26)

\hat{\ell}_{i}\geqslant k+N_{+},\qquad\text{ for }i=1,2,\ldots,\widehat{m}_{1}.

This lower bound will be used later in the proof.

Equipped with the above set of notation we now find that, for any
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
,

(5.27)

\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\eta_{i}(z)^{\widehat{N}_{+}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\eta_{i}(z)^{-\hat{\ell}_{i}}.

Thus we indeed have that
\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})
is constant
for
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
.
We now adopt a similar decomposition of the set of indices
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
so that again inside each of the blocks
\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k})
will remain the same. This necessitates the following notation: By a similar reasoning to the above the sum over
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
in (
5.20
) can be restricted to the following set

(5.28)

\widehat{L}_{k}^{(2)}:=\{\mathcal{Y}_{k}:\,1\leqslant y_{i^{\prime}+1,1}\leqslant y_{i^{\prime},1}<y_{i^{\prime}+1,2}\leqslant y_{i^{\prime},2}<\cdots<y_{i^{\prime}+1,k+N_{+}}\leqslant y_{i^{\prime},k+N_{+}}\leqslant\widehat{N}_{+}\}.

Next, for
{\bm{q}}:=(q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{\widehat{m}_{2}})
with
0\leqslant q_{i^{\prime}}\leqslant\widehat{N}_{+}
for
i^{\prime}\in[\widehat{m}_{2}]
we write

(5.29)

L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}:=\{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in\widehat{L}_{k}^{(2)}:\\
\,y_{i^{\prime}+1,1}+\sum_{j=2}^{k+N_{+}}(y_{i^{\prime}+1,j}-y_{i^{\prime},j-1})+(\widehat{N}_{+}-y_{i^{\prime},k+N_{+}})=q_{i^{\prime}}+k+N_{+},\text{ for }i^{\prime}\in[\widehat{m}_{2}]\}.

Using
[
8
, Lemma A.3]
we observe that for
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
,

(5.30)

\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k})=\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\eta_{i^{\prime}+\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)^{q_{i^{\prime}}}.

Now upon recalling the restriction on
X_{1}
and
Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}
from (
5.21
), we deduce from (
5.20
), (
5.27
), and (
5.30
) that, for any
X,Y\subset[N]
such that
|X|=|Y|=k
,
we have the representation

(5.31)

\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)=a_{-N_{-}}^{N-k}\cdot\Bigg[\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{k}^{(1)}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{k}^{(2)}}\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k})\cdot\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])\cdot\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k})\Bigg]\\
=a_{-N_{-}}^{N-k}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\eta_{i}(z)^{\widehat{N}_{+}}\cdot\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{\ell}},{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\eta_{i}(z)^{-\hat{\ell}_{i}}\\
\cdot\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])\cdot\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\eta_{i^{\prime}+\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)^{q_{i^{\prime}}}\cdot{\bf 1}_{X_{1}=X\cup[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N]}\cdot{\bf 1}_{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]}\Bigg].

5.2.
Bound on
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y)

In this section we state bounds on
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)
which will be used in Section
6.1
to compute high moments of the non-dominant terms in the expansion
\det(P_{z}^{\delta})
. While stating (and deriving) such bounds it will be convenient to scale
\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)
(and
\det_{k}(z)
) by the order of magnitude of the dominant term in the expansion of
\det(P_{z}^{\delta})
. Therefore, for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
we define

(5.32)

\mathfrak{K}(z)=\mathfrak{K}(z,d,\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}):=a_{-N_{-}}^{N}\cdot N^{-\gamma\widehat{d}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}\eta_{j}(z)^{N},

where
\widehat{m}_{1}
is as in (
5.12
) and

(5.33)

\widehat{d}=\widehat{d}(z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}d-{\sf g}_{0}&\mbox{ if }z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)},\\
d&\mbox{ if }z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}.\end{array}\right.

Recalling (
5.12
) and (
5.15
),
this implies that

(5.34)

\widehat{d}(\cdot)=\widehat{m}_{2}(\cdot)-N_{+}\qquad\text{ on }\mathcal{T}^{d}.

For
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
, further set

(5.35)

\widehat{\det}_{k}(z):={\det}_{k}(z)/\mathfrak{K}(z),\qquad k=0,1,2,\ldots,N,

and for any
X,Y\subset[N]
such that
|X|=|Y|=k
,

(5.36)

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}=\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z):=\frac{\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)}{N^{\gamma(\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+})}\cdot\mathfrak{K}(z)}=\frac{\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)}{a_{-N_{-}}^{N}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}\eta_{j}(z)^{N}},

where the last equality is due to
(
5.32
) and (
5.34
).

The following is the first main result of this subsection. Its proof spans over this and the next subsection.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
p(\cdot)
be a Laurent polynomial with
N_{+}\geqslant 0,N_{-}\geqslant 1

and
{\sf g}(p)={\sf g}_{0}\in\mathds{N}
. Fix
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}/\varepsilon_{0}
is sufficiently small. Fix
X,Y\subset[N]
such that
|X|=|Y|=k
so that

X=\{x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{k}\}
and

Y=\{y_{1}<y_{2}<\cdots<y_{k}\}.

Assume
\widehat{d}\geqslant 0
.
Then we have the following bounds on
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)
,
for all
z\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d}
.

(i)

There exists a constant
C_{\ref {lem:comb-bound-tube-1}}<\infty
depending only on
\varepsilon_{0}
and
p
so that for any

k\in\mathds{N}
satisfying
k+N_{+}\geqslant\widehat{m}_{2}
,

(5.37)

|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)|\leqslant C_{\ref {lem:comb-bound-tube-1}}^{\widehat{m}_{1}(k+N_{+})}\cdot|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|^{-NG(k,z)}\\
\cdot\left\{\sum_{q\geqslant 0}\binom{q(\widehat{m}_{2}+1)+\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\right)^{q}\cdot\mathfrak{I}({X,Y,q})\right\},

where

(5.38)

G(k,z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}&\mbox{ if }\widehat{m}_{2}\leqslant k+N_{+}<\widehat{m}_{2}+{\sf g}_{0}\mbox{ and }z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)},\\
{\sf g}_{0}&\mbox{ if }k+N_{+}\geqslant\widehat{m}_{2}+{\sf g}_{0}\mbox{ and }z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)},\\
0&\mbox{ if }z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)},\end{array}\right.

and

(5.39)

{\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,q)}:=\prod_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}}{\bf 1}_{\{y_{j}\leqslant q\}}\cdot\prod_{j=k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}+1}^{k}{\bf 1}_{\{N-x_{j}\leqslant q\}}.

(ii)

Let
k\in\mathds{N}
be such that
k+N_{+}<\widehat{m}_{2}
. Then,
for all large
N
,

|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)|\leqslant\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}\right)^{N}.

Remark 5.14
.

The roots of
p_{z}(\cdot)=0
can be partitioned into blocks of cardinality

{\sf g}(p)={\sf g}_{0}
such that all
{\sf g}_{0}
roots
in any of those blocks have the same modulus. One also has that
N_{+}
is a multiple of
{\sf g}_{0}
. Therefore,
d=m_{+}-N_{+}\geqslant 1
implies that

d\geqslant{\sf g}_{0}
. On the other hand, for
d=0
we have that
\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}=\emptyset
. Hence, whenever needed, we will use the bound on
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)
derived in Lemma
5.13
for all
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
and all
d\geqslant 0
.

Some explanations of the bounds stated in Lemma
5.13
are in order. We will see in Section
6
that, for
k_{0}\leqslant N
such that
k_{0}+N_{+}=\widehat{m}_{2}(z)
,
{\det}_{k_{0}}(z)
will be the dominant term in the expansion (
5.6
),
and
we will show in Corollary
6.13
that it has the same order of magnitude as that of
\mathfrak{K}(z)
. The other
{\det}_{k}(z)
will
be negligible compared to
{\det}_{k_{0}}(z)
.
Since the entries of the noise matrix are independent and of zero mean,
one gets from (
5.9
) that

(5.40)

\mathbf{E}[|{\det}_{k}(z)|^{2}]=N^{-2\gamma k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}|\mathfrak{D}(X,Y,z)|^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}(|\det(Q[X;Y])|^{2}).

Therefore, the bound in Lemma
5.13
(ii) indeed shows that, for
k
such that
k+N_{+}<\widehat{m}_{2}(z)
one has that
{\det}_{k}(z)
is
exponentially small
compared to
{\det}_{k_{0}}(z)
.

The implication of the bound in Lemma
5.13
(i) is a bit more delicate. Notice from (
5.37
)-(
5.39
) that the number of choices of the
largest
(\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+})
elements of
X
is bounded by
O(q^{\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}})
. Same holds for the smallest
(\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+})
elements of
Y
. Since there is a factor in (
5.37
) which is exponential in
q
those elements of
X
and
Y
are
essentially fixed
for the purpose of computation of the
second moment of
{\det}_{k}(z)
. This observation, as well as the factor
|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|^{-NG(k,z)}
in (
5.37
),
are crucial in determining the correct order of magnitude for
{\det}_{k}(z)
for
k>\widehat{m}_{2}(z)-N_{+}
. Now summing over the allowable ranges of the rest of the elements of
X
and
Y
, i.e. those which are
free
, using (
5.40
) one obtains that, for
k>\widehat{m}_{2}(z)-N_{+}
and
z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}
with
1<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
,
{\det}_{k}(z)
is
polynomially small
compared to
{\det}_{k_{0}}(z)
.
See the proof of Lemma
6.2
for details.

The proof of Lemma
5.13
requires two auxiliary lemmas. Before stating them we introduce the notation

(5.41)

{\bm{x}}_{i}={\bm{x}}_{i}(\mathcal{X}_{k}):=\sum_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}}x_{i,j};\text{ for }i\in[\widehat{m}_{1}+1],\,\text{ and }\,{\bm{y}}_{i^{\prime}}={\bm{y}}_{i^{\prime}}(\mathcal{Y}_{k}):=\sum_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}}y_{i^{\prime},j};\text{ for }i^{\prime}\in[\widehat{m}_{2}+1].

Below is the first auxiliary lemma. In its statement we use the notation
of Lemma
5.13
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Fix
{\bm{\ell}}\in[\widehat{N}_{+}]^{\widehat{m}_{1}}
and
{\bm{q}}\in[\widehat{N}_{+}]^{\widehat{m}_{2}}
.
For
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L^{(1)}_{{\bm{\ell}},k}
and

\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L^{(2)}_{{\bm{\ell}},k}
, set

(5.42)

\mathfrak{F}_{1}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k},{\bm{\ell}},z):=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\eta_{i}(z)^{-\hat{\ell}_{i}},

(5.43)

\mathfrak{F}_{2}=\mathfrak{F}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k},{\bm{q}},z):=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+i}(z)}^{q_{i}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k},{\bm{q}},z):=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\left(\frac{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+i}(z)}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)}\right)^{q_{i}}.

Assume
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}/3
. Then, for any
z\in\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d}
we have

(5.44)

\sum_{{\bm{\ell}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{1}\right|\cdot{\bf 1}_{\{X_{1}=X\cup([\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N])\}}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\widehat{m}_{1}(k+N_{+})},

(5.45)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{2}\right|\cdot{\bf 1}_{\{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]\}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{2(\widehat{m}_{2}+1)}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)+1},

and

(5.46)

\sum_{{\bm{q}}:\,\overline{\bm{q}}=q}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}\left|\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{2}\right|\cdot{\bf 1}_{\{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]\}}\leqslant\binom{q(\widehat{m}_{2}+1)+\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\right)^{q},

where

(5.47)

\overline{\bm{q}}=\overline{\bm{q}}({\bm{q}}):=\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}q_{i^{\prime}}.

Proof.

We first prove (
5.44
). We will iteratively sum over the collection of indices
\mathcal{X}_{k}
such that
X_{1}=X\cup([\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N])
, starting from
X_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}
. For
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L^{(1)}_{{\bm{\ell}},k}
we note that
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
depends on
\mathcal{X}_{k}
only through the values of
{\bm{\ell}}
. Thus, to compute the sum over
\mathcal{X}_{k}
such that
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L^{(1)}_{{\bm{\ell}},k}
it is enough to find a bound on the number of possible choices for all those indices.

In the first step we keep the collection of indices
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots X_{\widehat{m}_{1}})
frozen. Then, upon recalling (
5.25
) we observe that the number of choices of
\{x_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1,j}\}_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}}
is bounded above by
\binom{\hat{\ell}_{\widehat{m}_{1}}-1}{k+N_{+}-1}
. We iterate this argument for any
i\in[\widehat{m}_{1}]
. The number of choices of the indices
\{x_{i+1,j}\}_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}}
, upon keeping the collection of indices
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{i})
frozen, is at most
\binom{\hat{\ell}_{i}-1}{k+N_{+}-1}
.
This yields that

(5.48)

\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{1}\right|\cdot{\bf 1}_{X_{1}=X\cup([\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N])}\leqslant\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\binom{\hat{\ell}_{i}-1}{k+N_{+}-1}\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\hat{\ell}_{i}},

where we recalled (
5.12
) and used that for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
,

(5.49)

\max\left\{\max_{j=\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}^{\widetilde{m}}|\eta_{j}(z)|,\max_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}|\eta_{j}(z)|^{-1}\right\}=\max\left\{|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}(z)|,|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}}(z)|^{-1}\right\}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon_{0}.

Finally, to compute the sum over
\hat{\ell}_{1},\hat{\ell}_{2},\ldots,\hat{\ell}_{\widehat{m}_{1}}
we use the following combinatorial identity: For any
\uplambda\in(0,1)
and
m\in\mathds{N}

(5.50)

\sum_{s\geqslant m}\binom{s-1}{m-1}\cdot\uplambda^{s-m}=(1-\uplambda)^{-m}.

Indeed, applying the above identity with
\uplambda=(1-\varepsilon_{0})
,
m=k+N_{+}
and
s=\hat{\ell}_{i}
for
i=1,2,\ldots,\widehat{m}_{1}
we deduce (
5.44
). Note also that we also used the lower bound
\hat{\ell}_{i}\geqslant k+N_{+}
(see (
5.26
)).

We now turn to the proof of (
5.45
). We first prove the following intermediate step.

(5.51)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{q}}:\overline{\bm{q}}=q}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{2}\right|\cdot{\bf 1}_{\{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]\}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\binom{q(\widehat{m}_{2}+1)+\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}\left(1-{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{q}.

To obtain (
5.51
), we sum over the indices
\{Y_{i^{\prime}}\}_{i^{\prime}=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}
iteratively, starting with
Y_{1}
. It is straightforward to see that, upon keeping the other indices frozen, the number of choices for the indices
\{y_{1,j}\}_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}}
such that
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
is bounded above by
\binom{q_{1}+k+N_{+}}{k+N_{+}}
. We use the same reasoning to successively bound the number of allowable choices of
Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}}
. It yields that the number of choices of
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
such that
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
and
Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]
is bounded above by

\prod_{i^{\prime}=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\binom{q_{i^{\prime}}+k+N_{+}}{k+N_{+}}\leqslant\binom{\overline{\bm{q}}+k+N_{+}}{k+N_{+}}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\leqslant\binom{\widehat{m}_{2}(\overline{\bm{q}}+k+N_{+})}{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+})}.

Thus, by (
5.49
), we have that the
lhs
of (
5.51
) is bounded above by

(5.52)

\sum_{{\bm{q}}:\,\overline{\bm{q}}=q}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}\left|\mathfrak{F}_{2}\right|\cdot{\bf 1}_{\{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]\}}\leqslant\binom{\widehat{m}_{2}(q+k+N_{+})}{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+})}\cdot|\{{\bm{q}}:\,\overline{\bm{q}}=q\}|\cdot(1-\varepsilon_{0})^{q}.

Noting that, for any
q\geqslant 0
,

(5.53)

|\{{\bm{q}}:\,\overline{\bm{q}}=q\}|=\binom{q+\widehat{m}_{2}-1}{\widehat{m}_{2}-1}\leqslant\binom{q+\widehat{m}_{2}}{\widehat{m}_{2}},

that
\binom{a}{b}\binom{c}{d}\leqslant\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{b+d}\binom{a}{k^{\prime}}\binom{c}{b+d-k^{\prime}}=\binom{a+c}{b+d}
,
and substituting in
(
5.52
) we obtain (
5.51
). To derive (
5.45
) from (
5.51
) we observe that

(5.54)

(1-\varepsilon)\leqslant\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2m_{\star}}\right)^{m_{\star}},\qquad\text{ for }\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)\text{ and }m_{\star}\in\mathds{N},

and use (
5.50
) again.

Finally, to prove (
5.46
) applying (
5.49
) we find that whenever
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}/3
,

\sup_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}}\left\{\max_{i=\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}^{\widetilde{m}}\left|\frac{\eta_{i}(z)}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)}\right|\right\}\leqslant 1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2},

Repeating the proof of (
5.51
), the bound in (
5.46
) follows.
∎

The next lemma is
the second auxiliary result to be used in the proof of Lemma
5.13
, and uses its notation.

Lemma 5.16
.

Fix
X_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1},Y_{1}\subset[N+N_{+}]
such that
|X_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}|=|Y_{1}|=k+N_{+}
, where
k\leqslant N
.
If

(5.55)

x_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1,j}\geqslant y_{1,j};\,j\in[k+N_{+}],\quad\text{ and }\quad x_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1,j}<y_{1,j+{\sf g}_{0}};\,j\in[k+N_{+}-{\sf g}_{0}],

then, for all
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
,

(5.56)

\left|\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])\right|\leqslant{\sf g}_{0}^{(2+{\sf g}_{0})(k+N_{+})}\cdot|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|^{{\sf g}_{0}(N-k)+{\bm{y}}_{1}-{\bm{x}}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}}.

On the other hand, if (
5.55
) does not hold then the
lhs
of (
5.56
) vanishes.

The proof of Lemma
5.16
is postponed to Section
5.3
. In that section a Toeplitz minor would be represented as a certain
skew Schur polynomial
and using that representation Lemma
5.16
will be proved. We now prove Lemma
5.13
.

Proof of Lemma
5.13
(i) for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
.

Fix
k\in\mathds{N}
such that
k+N_{+}\geqslant\widehat{m}_{2}
. Fix
{\bm{\ell}},{\bm{q}},\mathcal{X}_{k}
, and
\mathcal{Y}_{k}
such that
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
, and (
5.21
) holds. We also assume that (
5.55
) holds, for otherwise by the second half of Lemma
5.16
there is no contribution to
the sum in (
5.31
).

We first show that for such pairs
(\mathcal{X}_{k},{\bm{\ell}})
and
(\mathcal{Y}_{k},{\bm{q}})
one must have

(5.57)

\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,\overline{\bm{q}})=1,

where we recall (
5.39
) and (
5.47
) for the definitions of
\mathfrak{I}
and
\overline{\bm{q}}
.
To see (
5.57
), as
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
, we note that for any
j\in[\widehat{m}_{2}]

(5.58)

y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1,j}=\sum_{i=0}^{j-2}(y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1-i,j-i}-y_{\widehat{m}_{2}-i,j-i-1})+y_{\widehat{m}_{2}-j+2,1}\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}q_{\widehat{m}_{2}-i}=\sum^{\widehat{m}_{2}}_{\lambda=\widehat{m}_{2}-j+1}q_{\lambda}\leqslant\overline{\bm{q}}.

Similarly, as
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
, using that (
5.55
) holds, we also observe that

(5.59)

(N+N_{+}-x_{1,j})\leqslant(\widehat{N}_{+}-x_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1,j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:xy-rel}}}{{\leqslant}}(\widehat{N}_{+}-y_{1,j})\\
=(\widehat{N}_{+}-y_{k+N_{+}+1-j,k+N_{+}})+\sum_{i=0}^{k+N_{+}-j-1}(y_{i+2,j+i+1}-y_{i+1,j+i})\leqslant\sum_{\lambda=1}^{k+N_{+}+1-j}q_{\lambda}\leqslant\overline{\bm{q}},

where in the last step we also used that

j\geqslant k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}+1\geqslant 1\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad k+N_{+}+1-j\leqslant\widehat{m}_{2}.

The inequalities (
5.58
)-(
5.59
) together with (
5.21
) now establish (
5.57
). This implies that while summing over the indices in (
5.31
), without loss of generality we can assume that (
5.57
) holds.

Before evaluating that sum we do some more simplifications to the representation in (
5.31
). As
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
, recalling the definition of
\{{\bm{x}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}
from (
5.41
) we see that

{\bm{x}}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}({\bm{x}}_{i+1}-{\bm{x}}_{i})+{\bm{x}}_{1}=\widehat{m}_{1}(k+N_{+})-\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\hat{\ell}_{i}+{\bm{x}}_{1}.

Thus, for any
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
,

(5.60)

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}=\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{k},{\bm{\ell}},z):=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\eta_{i}(z)^{-\hat{\ell}_{i}}\cdot\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)^{-{\bm{x}}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}}\\
=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\left(\frac{\eta_{i}(z)}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)}\right)^{-\hat{\ell}_{i}}\cdot\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)^{-\widehat{m}_{1}(k+N_{+})}\cdot\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)^{-{\bm{x}}_{1}}.

On the other hand, for any
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
,

{\bm{y}}_{1}={\bm{y}}_{2}-q_{1}+(N-k)={\bm{y}}_{3}+2(N-k)-q_{1}-q_{2}=\cdots={\bm{y}}_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}+\widehat{m}_{2}\cdot(N-k)-\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}q_{i}.

This yields that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}=\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{k},{\bm{\ell}},z)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+i}(z)^{q_{i}}\cdot\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)^{{\bm{y}}_{1}}

(5.61)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}}\left(\frac{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}+i}(z)}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)}\right)^{q_{i}}\cdot\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)^{\widehat{m}_{2}(N-k)+{\bm{y}}_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}}.

Fix
k
such that
k+N_{+}\geqslant\widehat{m}_{2}+{\sf g}_{0}
. For
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in\widehat{L}_{k}^{(2)}
, upon using that
\widehat{m}_{2}\geqslant 0
and (
5.55
), we find that

\displaystyle{\bm{y}}_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bm-x}}}{{\geqslant}}\sum_{j=\widehat{m}_{2}+{\sf g}_{0}+1}^{k+N_{+}}y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1,j}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:L-k-Y}}}{{\geqslant}}\sum_{j={\sf g}_{0}+1}^{k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}}y_{1,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:xy-rel}}}{{\geqslant}}\sum_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}-{\sf g}_{0}}x_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1,j}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:hat-ell-lbd}}}{{\geqslant}}\sum_{j=1}^{k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}-{\sf g}_{0}}x_{1,j}-\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\hat{\ell}_{i}+(k+N_{+})\widehat{m}_{1}

(5.62)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:bm-x}}}{{\geqslant}}{\bm{x}}_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\hat{\ell}_{i}+(k+N_{+})\widehat{m}_{1}-\widehat{N}_{+}(\widehat{m}_{2}+{\sf g}_{0}).

Note that for
k\in\mathds{N}
such that
\widehat{m}_{2}\leqslant k+N_{+}\leqslant\widehat{m}_{2}+{\sf g}_{0}
, the sum over
j
in the left side of (
5.2
) is
empty. Therefore, by (
5.26
) and as
X_{1}\subset[\widehat{N}_{+}]
we obtain that for such values of
k
,

(5.63)

\displaystyle{\bm{y}}_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}

\displaystyle\geqslant{\bm{x}}_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}\hat{\ell}_{i}+(k+N_{+})\widehat{m}_{1}-\widehat{N}_{+}(k+N_{+}).

Now as
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
we have
|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|\leqslant 1
. Thus, recalling (
5.42
)-(
5.43
) and (
5.38
), and using (
5.2
)-(
5.63
) we deduce that

(5.64)

|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}|\cdot|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}|\leqslant|\mathfrak{F}_{1}|\cdot|\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{2}|\cdot|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|^{-NG(k,z)-\widetilde{m}(k+N_{+})}.

On the other hand, from (
5.60
)-(
5.2
), (
5.31
), and (
5.36
) it follows that

(5.65)

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant|a_{-N_{-}}|^{-k}\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}|\eta_{i}(z)|^{N_{+}}\prod_{i=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}|\eta_{i}(z)|^{-N}\cdot\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{\ell}},{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}|

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\left|\frac{\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)^{{\bm{y}}_{1}-{\bm{x}}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}}}\right|\cdot|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{2}|\cdot{\bf 1}_{X_{1}=X\cup[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N]}\cdot{\bf 1}_{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]}\cdot\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,\overline{\bm{q}})\Bigg],

where we also used that
{\sf g}(p)={\sf g}_{0}
(recall
(
1.6
) for the
definition of
{\sf g}(\cdot)
) implies that

(5.66)

|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|=|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+2}(z)|=\cdots=|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|.

It is clear that if
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
for some
d
then
z
lies in compact domain in
\mathds{C}
. As the map
z\mapsto\max_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}|\eta_{j}(z)|
is
continuous, we therefore
have that

\max_{d}\sup_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}}\max_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}|\eta_{j}(z)|<\infty.

Therefore, continuing from above, applying Lemma
5.16
, from (
5.57
) and (
5.64
) we deduce that, there exists some constant
\widetilde{C}_{\ref {lem:comb-bound-tube-1}}<\infty
such that

\left|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)\right|\leqslant\widetilde{C}_{\ref {lem:comb-bound-tube-1}}^{\widehat{m}_{1}(k+N_{+})}\cdot|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|^{-NG(k,z)-\widetilde{m}(k+N_{+})}\cdot\\
\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{\ell}},{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}|\mathfrak{F}_{1}|\cdot|\widetilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{2}|\cdot{\bf 1}_{X_{1}=X\cup[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N]}\cdot{\bf 1}_{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]}\cdot\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,\overline{\bm{q}})\Bigg].

Finally we use Lemma
5.15
and

(5.67)

1-\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant\min^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}_{j=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}|\eta_{j}(z)|=\max^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}_{j=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}|\eta_{j}(z)|\leqslant 1,\quad\mbox{\rm for $z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}$},

to derive the desired bound. This completes the proof of
Lemma
5.13
(i) for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.13
(ii) for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
.

As
k+N_{+}<\widehat{m}_{2}
, for any
\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
we have that

\displaystyle\overline{\bm{q}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:ol-bm-q}}}{{\geqslant}}\sum_{i=1}^{k+N_{+}+1}q_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:L-ell-k2}}}{{\geqslant}}y_{2,1}+\sum_{j=2}^{k+N_{+}}(y_{j+1,j}-y_{j,j-1})+(\widehat{N}_{+}-y_{k+N_{+}+1,k+N_{+}})-(k+N_{+})(k+N_{+}+1)

(5.68)

\displaystyle\geqslant\widehat{N}_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}^{2},

where in the second inequality we used that

y_{2,1}\leqslant q_{1}+k+N_{+},y_{3,2}-y_{2,1}\leqslant q_{2}+k+N_{+},\ldots,\widehat{N}_{+}-y_{k+N_{+}+1,k+N_{+}}\leqslant q_{k+N_{+}+1}+k+N_{+},

and in the last we telescoped the sum and used again that
k+N_{+}<\widehat{m}_{2}
.
This implies that the set
L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}
is empty unless (
5.68
) holds.

On the other hand, recalling (
5.42
)-(
5.43
), and using (
5.31
) and (
5.36
) we note that

\left|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)\right|\leqslant|a_{-N_{-}}|^{-k}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}|\eta_{i}(z)|^{N_{+}}\cdot\prod_{i=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}|\eta_{i}(z)|^{-N}\cdot\\
\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{\ell}},{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}|\mathfrak{F}_{1}|\cdot\left|{\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])}\right|\cdot|\mathfrak{F}_{2}|\cdot{\bf 1}_{X_{1}=X\cup[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N]}\cdot{\bf 1}_{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]}\Bigg].

From Lemma
5.16
we have that

(5.69)

\left|\frac{\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])}{\prod_{i=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}\eta_{i}(z)^{N}}\right|\leqslant{\sf g}_{0}^{({\sf g}_{0}+2)(k+N_{+})}|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|^{{\bm{y}}_{1}-{\bm{x}}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}-k{\sf g}_{0}}\leqslant(1+2\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})^{\widehat{m}_{2}N},

for all large
N
, where in the first step we have used (
5.66
) and in the second nequality we used (
5.67
),
that
{\bm{y}}_{1}\geqslant 0
,
{\bm{x}}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}\leqslant\widehat{m}_{2}\widehat{N}_{+}
,
and
k+N_{+}\leqslant\widehat{m}_{2}
.

Therefore, applying Lemma
5.15
,
and (
5.68
)-(
5.69
), we deduce that

(5.70)

\left|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:toep-minor-bd-21},\eqref{eq:wt-gD-bd-1}}}{{\leqslant}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\widehat{m}_{1}(k+N_{+})}\cdot(1+2\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})^{\widehat{m}_{2}N}\cdot\left[\sum_{{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}|\mathfrak{F}_{2}|\cdot{\bf 1}_{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]}\right]\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:wh-gD-bd-2}}}{{\leqslant}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\widehat{m}_{1}(k+N_{+})}\cdot(1+2\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})^{\widehat{m}_{2}N}\cdot\left[\sum_{\overline{\bm{q}}}\binom{\overline{\bm{q}}(\widehat{m}_{2}+1)+\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\right)^{\overline{\bm{q}}}\cdot{\bf 1}_{\{\overline{\bm{q}}\geqslant\widehat{N}_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}^{2}\}}\right].

Since
for any fixed
n_{1}\in\mathds{N}
and
n_{2}\in\mathds{N}
large enough, depending only on
\varepsilon_{0}
and
n_{1}
, one has

\sum_{\overline{\bm{q}}\geqslant n_{2}}\overline{\bm{q}}^{n_{1}}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\right)^{\overline{\bm{q}}}\leqslant\left(1-\frac{3\varepsilon_{0}}{8}\right)^{n_{2}},

and
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}/\varepsilon_{0}
is sufficiently small, upon using (
5.53
), the claimed bound on
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)
now follows from (
5.70
). This completes the proof of part (ii) for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
.
∎

Next we prove Lemma
5.13
for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
. It requires some minor modifications compared to the case
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.13
for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
.

First let us prove part (i). We begin by noting that (
5.57
) continues to holds even for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
and
k\leqslant N
such that
k+N_{+}\geqslant\widehat{m}_{2}
. Recall (
5.42
)-(
5.43
). We use (
5.31
) and (
5.36
) to note that

(5.71)

\left|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)\right|\\
\leqslant|a_{-N_{-}}|^{-k}\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}|\eta_{i}(z)|^{N_{+}}\prod_{i=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}|\eta_{i}(z)|^{-N}\cdot\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{\ell}},{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}|\mathfrak{F}_{1}|\cdot\left|{\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])}\right|\cdot|\mathfrak{F}_{2}|\\
\cdot{\bf 1}_{X_{1}=X\cup[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N]}\cdot{\bf 1}_{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]}\cdot\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,{\bm{q}})\Bigg].

As
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
implies that
|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|\geqslant 1
, applying Lemma
5.16
, and using that
{\bm{y}}_{1}\leqslant{\bm{x}}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}
, we obtain that

(5.72)

\left|\frac{\det(\mathcal{P}_{z}[\check{X}_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1};\check{Y}_{1}])}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)^{N{\sf g}_{0}}}\right|\leqslant{\sf g}_{0}^{{\sf g}_{0}+2(k+N_{+})}.

Thus, from (
5.71
), we now derive that

\left|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)\right|\leqslant|a_{-N_{-}}|^{-k}\cdot{\sf g}_{0}^{{\sf g}_{0}+2(k+N_{+})}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}}|\eta_{i}(z)|^{N_{+}}\cdot\\
\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{\ell}},{\bm{q}}}\sum_{\mathcal{X}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{\ell}},k}^{(1)}}\sum_{\mathcal{Y}_{k}\in L_{{\bm{q}},k}^{(2)}}|\mathfrak{F}_{1}|\cdot|\mathfrak{F}_{2}|\cdot{\bf 1}_{X_{1}=X\cup[\widehat{N}_{+}]\setminus[N]}\cdot{\bf 1}_{Y_{\widehat{m}_{2}+1}=(Y+N_{+})\cup[N_{+}]}\cdot\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,{\bm{q}})\Bigg].

The claimed bound on
\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)
now follows upon using Lemma

5.15
, in the same way as for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
. This completes the proof of part (i) of the lemma for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
.

We now turn to the proof of the second part. To this end, we note that all the steps of the proof of Lemma
5.13
(ii) continues to hold under the current setup, except for the bound (
5.69
). In fact, under our current setup, the bound (
5.69
) can be improved to (
5.72
). Thus, repeating rest of the arguments in the proof of Lemma
5.13
(ii) used for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
,
the proof of the second part of this lemma completes for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
.
∎

5.3.
Toeplitz minors as skew Schur polynomials

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
5.16
. This will follow from the following bound on minors of finitely banded Toeplitz matrices.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
{\sf g}_{\star},\widehat{N}\in\mathds{N}
. Fix a compact domain
\mathds{D}\subset\mathds{C}
and let
z\in\mathds{D}
. Let
\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)=\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(\widehat{p}_{z})
be the
\widehat{N}\times\widehat{N}
Toeplitz matrix with symbol

(5.73)

\widehat{p}_{z}(\tau):=\sum_{j=0}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\widehat{a}_{-j}(z)\tau^{-j},\quad a_{0}(z),a_{-1}(z),\ldots,a_{-{\sf g}_{\star}}(z)\in\mathds{C};\,a_{0}(z)\neq 0,\,a_{-{\sf g}_{\star}}(z)=1\,\forall z\in\mathds{D}.

Denote
\{\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z)\}_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}
to be the roots of the polynomial
\widehat{p}_{z}(\cdot)=0
. Assume that, for some constant
c_{1}\in(0,1)
,

(5.74)

\inf_{z\in\mathds{D}}\min_{k\neq j}\left|1-\frac{\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z)}{\widehat{\eta}_{k}(z)}\right|\geqslant c_{1}.

Then for any

\mathfrak{X}:=\{\mathfrak{x}_{1}<\mathfrak{x}_{2}<\cdots<\mathfrak{x}_{n}\}\subset[\widehat{N}]\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{Y}:=\{\mathfrak{y}_{1}<\mathfrak{y}_{2}<\cdots<\mathfrak{y}_{n}\}\subset[\widehat{N}]

we have that

(5.75)

\left|\det(\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)[\check{\mathfrak{X}},\check{\mathfrak{Y}}])\right|\\
\leqslant c_{1}^{-{\sf g}_{\star}n}{\sf g}_{\star}^{2n}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}|\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z)|^{\widehat{N}-n}\cdot\left(\min_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}|\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z)|\right)^{\bar{\mathfrak{y}}-\bar{\mathfrak{x}}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\bf 1}_{\{\mathfrak{x}_{i}\geqslant\mathfrak{y}_{i}\}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-{\sf g}_{\star}}{\bf 1}_{\{\mathfrak{x}_{i}<\mathfrak{y}_{i+{\sf g}_{\star}}\}},

where
\check{\mathfrak{X}}:=[\widehat{N}]\setminus\mathfrak{X}
,
\check{\mathfrak{Y}}:=[\widehat{N}]\setminus\mathfrak{Y}
,

\bar{\mathfrak{x}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{x}_{i},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bar{\mathfrak{y}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{y}_{i}.

Remark 5.18
.

The bound on the order of magnitude of Toeplitz minors derived via Proposition
5.17
can be seen to be optimal when the roots of
\widehat{p}_{z}(\cdot)=0
have the same moduli. However, it is suboptimal when the roots have different moduli. For example, consider the case
{\sf g}_{\star}=2
and choose
z\in\mathds{C}
such that
|\widehat{\eta}_{1}(z)|/|\widehat{\eta}_{2}(z)|\geqslant(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
for some
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
.
To see the sub optimality of the bound now let
\mathfrak{X}=\{\widehat{N}-1,\widehat{N}\}
and
\mathfrak{Y}=\{1,2\}
.

Since we will apply Proposition
5.17
for a Toeplitz matrix for which the roots of its symbols have the
same moduli, we have not tried to derive a version of Proposition
5.17
that captures the optimal order of magnitude even when roots have different moduli.

As already mentioned in Section
5.2
,
to prove Proposition
5.17
(and hence Lemma
5.16
) we will use the fact a Toeplitz minor can be expressed as certain
skew Schur polynomial
,
see Remark
5.25
below for historical
background. To state the relevant result and carry out the proof of Proposition
5.17
we need to borrow some notations from the theory of symmetric functions. The references
[
46
,
68
]
are excellent resources for this purpose.

We start with the definition of
complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials
.

Definition 5.19

(Complete homogeneous symmetric polynomial)
.

Fix
w,{\sf r}\in\mathds{N}
. The
{\sf r}
-th complete homogeneous polynomial in
w
variables, to be denoted by
{\sf h}_{\sf r}(\cdot)
, is the sum of all monomials of total degree
{\sf r}
. That is, for
(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{w})\in\mathds{R}^{n}
,

{\sf h}_{\sf r}(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{w}):=\sum_{1\leqslant s_{1}\leqslant s_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant s_{{\sf r}}\leqslant w}\prod_{u=1}^{{\sf r}}t_{s_{u}}.

We further set
{\sf h}_{0}:=1
and
{\sf h}_{{\sf r}}:=0
for
{\sf r}<0
.

We next proceed to define the notion of skew Schur polynomials.

Definition 5.20

(Skew partitions and skew Schur polynomials)
.

Fix
w,m\in\mathds{N}
. A vector

{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{m})
with

\lambda_{i}\in\mathds{Z}
,
i\in[m]
, and
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{m}\geqslant 0
is called an integer partition (or in short a partition), of length

\ell({\bm{\lambda}})=m
and weight

|{\bm{\lambda}}|=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}
.

For two partitions
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\lambda}}
we write
{\bm{\mu}}\subseteq{\bm{\lambda}}
if
\ell({\bm{\mu}})\leqslant\ell({\bm{\lambda}})
and
\mu_{s}\leqslant\lambda_{s}
for all
s\in[\ell({\bm{\mu}})]
. In that case, the pair
({\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}})
is called a skew partition and is often denoted by
{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}
.

Given a skew partition
{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}
we define the skew Schur polynomial in
w
variables as follows:

(5.76)

{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\cdot):=\det\left[({\sf h}_{\lambda_{u}-\mu_{v}-u+v}(\cdot))_{u,v=1}^{\ell({\bm{\lambda}})}\right],

where
\{{\sf h}_{\cdot}(\cdot)\}
are the complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials in
w
variables as in Definition
5.19
, and the partition
{\bm{\mu}}
is extended to have length
\ell({\bm{\lambda}})
by appending zeros. When
{\bm{\mu}}
is an empty partition the polynomial
{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/\emptyset}
is called a Schur polynomial and is written as
{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}}}
. Thus, for any given partition
{\bm{\lambda}}
, we have

(5.77)

{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}}}=\det\left[({\sf h}_{\lambda_{u}-u+v})_{u,v=1}^{\ell({\bm{\lambda}})}\right].

Remark 5.21
.

Skew Schur polynomials admit a combinatorial description in terms
of skew semistandard Young tableaux.
The equivalence of these two definitions is due to the first Jacobi-Trudi identity (see
[
68
, Theorem 7.16.1]
). Since we do not require the notion of Young tableaux elsewhere in this paper we have chosen (
5.76
) as the definition of the skew Schur polynomial
{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}
.
We refer the reader to
[
68
, Chapter 7.10]
for a
detailed overview of these matters.

The following identity, known already to Jacobi (see
[
68
, Exercise 7.4]
or
[
18
, Theorem 3.2]
)
provides an efficient representation of complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
{\sf r},w\in\mathds{N}
and
(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{w})\in\mathds{R}^{n}
. If
\{t_{s}\}_{s=1}^{w}
are pairwise distinct then

{\sf h}_{{\sf r}}(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{w})=\sum_{j=1}^{w}\frac{t_{j}^{{\sf r}+w-1}}{\prod_{k\in[w]\setminus\{j\}}(t_{j}-t_{k})}.

The next lemma, which is a key to the proof of Proposition
5.17
, states that a Toeplitz minor can be expressed in terms of skew Schur polynomials.

Lemma 5.23

(
[
47
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Consider the setup of Proposition
5.17
. Then

\det(\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)[\check{\mathfrak{X}};\check{\mathfrak{Y}}])=(-1)^{|{\bm{\lambda}}_{0}|+|{\bm{\mu}}_{0}|}\cdot\widehat{a}_{0}(z)^{\widehat{N}-n}\cdot{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}/{\bm{\mu}}_{0}}(\widehat{\eta}_{1}(z)^{-1},\widehat{\eta}_{2}(z)^{-1},\ldots,\widehat{\eta}_{{\sf g}_{*}}(z)^{-1}),

where

{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}:=(\widehat{N}-n+1-\mathfrak{y}_{1},\widehat{N}-n+2-\mathfrak{y}_{2},\ldots,\widehat{N}-\mathfrak{y}_{n})\text{ and }{\bm{\mu}_{0}}:=(\widehat{N}-n+1-\mathfrak{x}_{1},\widehat{N}-n+2-\mathfrak{x}_{2},\ldots,\widehat{N}-\mathfrak{x}_{n}).

Remark 5.24
.

The reader may note that the expression for the Toeplitz minor stated in Lemma
5.23
is somewhat different than the one in
[
47
, Theorem 2.1]
. This is due to the fact that
[
47
]
expresses the minor in terms of the roots of the polynomial
\overline{p}_{z}(\tau):=\tau^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\cdot\widehat{p}_{z}(\tau)
, where
\widehat{p}_{z}(\cdot)
is as in (
5.73
), while
\{\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z)\}_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}
are the roots of
\widehat{p}_{z}(\cdot)=0
which are indeed the reciprocals of those of
\overline{p}_{z}(\cdot)=0
.

Remark 5.25
.

It was first noted by Bump and Diaconis
[
17
]
that any Toeplitz minor can be expressed in terms of skew Schur polynomials. They expressed such minors as an integral of certain functions involving skew Schur polynomials over the unitary group (see
[
17
, Theorem 3]
). Later Alexandersson in
[
1
]
expressed minors of triangular Toeplitz matrices as certain skew Schur polynomials (see
[
1
, Proposition 3]
). Maximenko and Moctezuma-Salazar
[
47
]
gave
a different proof of such a representaion
for non-triangular banded Toeplitz matrices. Here we use the formulation of
[
47
]
which is the most convenient for our setup.

Before proving Proposition
5.17
we make the following elementary observation.

Lemma 5.26
.

Consider the setup of Proposition
5.17
. If
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}\subset[\widehat{N}]
are such that either there exists an
i\in[n]
for which
\mathfrak{x}_{i}<\mathfrak{y}_{i}
or there exists an
i\in[n-{\sf g}_{\star}]
such that
\mathfrak{x}_{i}\geqslant\mathfrak{y}_{i+{\sf g}_{\star}}
, then

\det(\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)[\check{\mathfrak{X}};\check{\mathfrak{Y}}])=0.

Proof.

We note that
\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)
is an upper triangular Toeplitz matrix with the symbol
\widehat{p}_{z}(\cdot)
(see (
5.73
)). As
\{\widehat{\eta}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}
are the roots of
\widehat{p}(1/\cdot)=0
, it follows that

\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)=\prod_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}(J_{\widehat{N}}-\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z)I_{\widehat{N}}).

Therefore, by the Cauchy-Binet theorem we have that

(5.78)

\det(\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)[\check{\mathfrak{X}};\check{\mathfrak{Y}}])=\sum_{\ell=2}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z_{\ell}\subset[\widehat{N}]\\
|Z_{\ell}|=n\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\det((J_{\widehat{N}}-\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z)I_{\widehat{N}})[\check{Z}_{j};\check{Z}_{j+1}]),

where we set
Z_{1}=\mathfrak{X}
and
Z_{{\sf g}_{\star}+1}=\mathfrak{Y}
. For
\ell\in[{\sf g}_{\star}+1]
, we index the elements of
Z_{\ell}
as follows:

Z_{\ell}:=\{z_{\ell,1}<z_{\ell,2}<\cdots<z_{\ell,n}\}.

By
[
8
, Lemma A.3]
we note that

\det((J_{\widehat{N}}-\widehat{\eta}_{\ell}(z)I_{\widehat{N}})[\check{Z}_{\ell};\check{Z}_{\ell+1}])\neq 0

if and only if

(5.79)

z_{\ell+1,1}\leqslant z_{\ell,1}<z_{\ell+1,2}\leqslant z_{\ell,2}<\cdots<z_{\ell+1,n}\leqslant z_{\ell,n}\leqslant n.

Therefore, in (
5.78
) we can restrict the sum over
\{Z_{2},Z_{3},\ldots,Z_{{\sf g}_{\star}}\}
such that (
5.79
) holds for all
\ell\in[{\sf g}_{\star}]
. On the other hand, if (
5.79
) holds for all
\ell\in[{\sf g}_{\star}]
, as
Z_{1}=\mathfrak{X}
and
Z_{{\sf g}_{\star}+1}=\mathfrak{Y}
, we derive that for all
i\in[n]

\mathfrak{y}_{i}=z_{{\sf g}_{\star}+1,i}\leqslant z_{{\sf g}_{\star},i}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant z_{1,i}=\mathfrak{x}_{i},

and for any
i\in[n-{\sf g}_{\star}]

\mathfrak{x}_{i}=z_{1,i}<z_{2,i+1}<z_{3,i+2}<\cdots<z_{{\sf g}_{\star}+1,i+{\sf g}_{\star}}=\mathfrak{y}_{i+{\sf g}_{\star}}.

Therefore, if either of the above two conditions are violated for some
i\in[n]
, then there must be an
\ell\in[{\sf g}_{\star}]
such that (
5.79
) is violated. In that case, the sum
\{Z_{2},Z_{3},\ldots,Z_{{\sf g}_{\star}}\}
in (
5.78
) is an empty sum and hence
\det(\widehat{T}_{\widehat{N}}(z)[\check{\mathfrak{X}};\check{\mathfrak{Y}}])=0
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
5.17
.

Proof of Proposition
5.17
.

From (
5.76
) we see that

(5.80)

{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}/{\bm{\mu}}_{0}}(\cdot)=\det\left[\left({\sf h}_{\mathfrak{x}_{v}-\mathfrak{y}_{u}}(\cdot)\right)_{u,v=1}^{n}\right],

where
{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}
and
{\bm{\mu}_{0}}
are as in Lemma
5.23
. Set
\underline{\widehat{\xi}}:=(\widehat{\xi}_{1},\widehat{\xi}_{2},\ldots,\widehat{\xi}_{{\sf g}_{\star}})
, where
\widehat{\xi}_{i}:=\widehat{\eta}_{i}^{-1}(z)
for
i\in[n]
. Using
(
5.80
) in the first equality and
Lemma
5.22
in the second,
we have that

(5.81)

{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}/{\bm{\mu}}_{0}}(\underline{\widehat{\xi}})=\sum_{\uppi}\operatorname{sgn}(\uppi)\prod_{v=1}^{n}{\sf h}_{\mathfrak{x}_{v}-\mathfrak{y}_{\uppi(v)}}(\underline{\widehat{\xi}})\\
=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{n}=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\prod_{u=1}^{n}\frac{\widehat{\xi}_{j_{u}}^{{\sf g}_{*}-1}}{\prod_{k_{u}\in[{\sf g}_{\star}]\setminus\{j_{u}\}}(\widehat{\xi}_{j_{u}}-\widehat{\xi}_{k_{u}})}\cdot\left[\sum_{\uppi}\operatorname{sgn}(\uppi)\cdot\prod_{v=1}^{n}\widehat{\xi}_{j_{v}}^{\mathfrak{x}_{v}-\mathfrak{y}_{\uppi(v)}}\cdot\prod_{v=1}^{n}{\bf 1}_{\{\mathfrak{x}_{v}\geqslant\mathfrak{y}_{\uppi(v)}\}}\right],

where the sum in the first step and the innermost sum in the second step are over all permutations
\uppi
on
[n]
, and in the last step we also used the fact that
{\sf h}_{\sf r}(\cdot)=0
for
{\sf r}<0
.

We claim that there are at most
{\sf g}_{\star}^{n}
many permutations
\uppi
such that the summand in the innermost sum in (
5.81
) is nonzero. That is, there are at most
{\sf g}_{\star}^{n}
many permutations
\uppi
such that

(5.82)

\mathfrak{x}_{v}\geqslant\mathfrak{y}_{\uppi(v)},\quad\text{ for all }v\in[n].

Equipped with this claim, upon crudely bounding each term appearing in the sums in (
5.81
) by its maximum, it now follows that

(5.83)

\left|{\bm{s}}_{{\bm{\lambda}_{0}}/{\bm{\mu}}_{0}}(\underline{\widehat{\xi}})\right|\leqslant{\sf g}_{\star}^{2n}\left(\max_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\left\{\prod_{k\in[{\sf g}_{\star}]\setminus\{j\}}\left|\frac{1}{1-(\widehat{\xi}_{k}/\widehat{\xi}_{j})}\right|\right\}\right)^{n}\cdot\left(\max_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}|\widehat{\xi}_{j}|\right)^{\bar{\mathfrak{x}}-\bar{\mathfrak{y}}}\leqslant{\sf g}_{\star}^{2n}\cdot c_{1}^{-{\sf g}_{\star}n}\cdot\left(\max_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}|\widehat{\xi}_{j}|\right)^{\bar{\mathfrak{x}}-\bar{\mathfrak{y}}},

where the last inequality is due to (
5.74
).
Now, using Lemmas
5.23
and
5.26
, and noting that

(5.84)

\widehat{a}_{0}(z)=(-1)^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\prod_{j=1}^{{\sf g}_{\star}}\widehat{\eta}_{j}(z),

we obtain (
5.75
)
from (
5.83
).

Thus, to finish the proof it remains to prove the claim regarding the number of permutations
\uppi
satisfying (
5.82
). To do that, by Lemma
5.26
, without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathfrak{x}_{v}<\mathfrak{y}_{v+{\sf g}_{\star}}
for all
v\in[n-{\sf g}_{\star}]
.

Now we bound the number of choices of
\uppi
satisfying (
5.82
) as follows:

•

As
\mathfrak{x}_{1}<\mathfrak{y}_{1+{\sf g}_{\star}}
,
\mathfrak{y}_{1}<\mathfrak{y}_{2}<\cdots<\mathfrak{y}_{n}
, and
\mathfrak{x}_{1}\geqslant\mathfrak{y}_{\uppi(1)}
we find that
\uppi(1)\in[{\sf g}_{\star}]
. That is, the number of choices for
\uppi(1)
is
{\sf g}_{\star}
.

•

Having chosen
\uppi(1)
we now choose
\uppi(2)
. Using the same argument as above we note that
\uppi(2)\in[{\sf g}_{\star}+1]
. As
\uppi(1)\in[{\sf g}_{\star}]\subset[{\sf g}_{\star}+1]
the number of choices for
\uppi(2)
is
again at most
{\sf g}_{\star}
.

•

Continuing from the above, we find that for any given
v\in[n-{\sf g}_{\star}]
we must have
\uppi(v)\in[v+{\sf g}_{\star}-1]
. On the other hand
\uppi(1),\uppi(2),\ldots,\uppi(v-1)\in[v+{\sf g}_{\star}-2]
. Thus the number of choices for
\uppi(v)
is at most
{\sf g}_{\star}
.

•

Finally
\uppi(n-{\sf g}_{\star}+1),\uppi(n-{\sf g}_{\star}+2),\ldots,\uppi(n)
can be chosen in at most
{\sf g}_{\star}!(\leqslant{\sf g}_{\star}^{{\sf g}_{\star}})
ways.

This proves the claim and hence we have the desired bound.
∎

We end this section with the proof of Lemma
5.16
. This is a direct consequence of Proposition
5.17
.

Proof of Lemma
5.16
.

We recall from (
5.13
) that
\mathcal{P}_{z}
is an upper triangular Toeplitz matrix of dimension
\widehat{N}_{+}
such that the roots of its symbols are
\{-\eta_{j}(z)\}_{j=\widehat{m}_{1}+1}^{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}
. Note that
{\sf g}(p)={\sf g}_{0}
(see (
1.6
) for a definition of
{\sf g}(\cdot)
) implies that there exists
\widetilde{\eta}(z)\in\mathds{C}
such that

(5.85)

\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+j}(z)=\widetilde{\eta}(z)\cdot e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}j/{\sf g}_{0}},\qquad j\in[{\sf g}_{0}].

Upon recalling the definition of the tube
\mathcal{T}^{d}
we see that

1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{3}\leqslant\inf_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}}|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|\leqslant\sup_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}}|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|\leqslant 1+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{3}.

This further implies that for any
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d}
we have that
\widetilde{\eta}(z)\neq 0
. Therefore, we indeed have that

(5.86)

\min_{i\neq j\in[{\sf g}_{0}]}\left|\frac{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+i}(z)}{\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+j}(z)}-1\right|\geqslant{\sf g}_{0}^{-1}.

Recall also, see e.g. (
5.66
), that

|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|=|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+2}(z)|=\cdots=|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+{\sf g}_{0}}(z)|.

As
|X_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}|=|Y_{1}|=k+N_{+}
the bound (
5.56
), as well as the fact that that the
lhs
of (
5.56
) equals zero when (
5.55
) is violated, is now immediate from Proposition
5.17
.
This completes the proof.
∎

6.
Proofs of the separation Theorems
5.4
and
5.7

In this section, we complete the proofs of Theorems
5.4
and
5.7
, based on
Lemma
5.13
.
The proof is split into four parts: first, upon employing Lemma
5.13
, we derive correct order of magnitudes (of moments) of

{\det}_{k}(z)
, per fixed
z
and
k
corresponding to non-dominant terms,
in the regions of interest. Next, in order to control

\sup_{z}\det_{k}(z)
, we
derive bounds on the moments on the supremum of the derivative of
\det_{k}(z)
.
Combining these steps
and
a covering argument, we get the desired bounds for
\sup_{z}\det_{k}(z)

for non-dominant terms in the expansion (
5.6
). The third part of the proof derives a uniform lower bound on the dominant term, using
Assumption
1.2
. In the last step,
we combine the above ingredients with
geometric information on the forbidden regions
to deduce Theorems
5.4
and
5.7
.

6.1.
Step 1: moment
bounds on the non-dominant terms (per fixed
z
)

In this section we derive bounds on the moments of
{\det}_{k}(z)
.
Recall (
5.6
),
(
5.35
), (
5.12
), and (
5.15
), and note that
\widehat{m}_{1}
and
\widehat{m}_{2}
(which depend on
z
)
are constant on the tubes
\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}
and
\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
with fixed
d
.
We first consider the case of
small

k
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let Assumption
1.1
hold.
Fix
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}/\varepsilon_{0}
is
sufficiently small. Fix
d\geqslant 0
and
s\in\{1,2\}
. Then, for all large
N
,

(6.1)

\max_{k<\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}}\sup_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(s)}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)\right|^{2}\right]\leqslant\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{8}\right)^{N}.

Proof.

Fix
s\in\{1,2\}
. Since the entries of
Q
are independent with zero mean and bounded variance, we get that for any
\mathbb{X}_{*},\mathbb{Y}_{*},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}\subset[N]

(6.2)

\mathbf{E}\left[\det(Q[\mathbb{X}_{*};\mathbb{Y}_{*}])\cdot\overline{\det(Q[\mathbb{X}^{\prime};\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}])}\right]\leqslant\mathfrak{C}_{1}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}k!&\mbox{if }\mathbb{X}_{*}=\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\,\mathbb{Y}_{*}=\mathbb{Y}^{\prime},\text{ and }|\mathbb{X}_{*}|=|\mathbb{Y}_{*}|=k,\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Therefore, upon recalling (
5.9
), (
5.35
)-(
5.36
), and (
5.40
) we find that

(6.3)

\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)\right|^{2}\right]\leqslant\mathfrak{C}_{1}N^{2\gamma(\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}-k)}\cdot k!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)|^{2}.

Since there are at most
\binom{N}{k}^{2}
choices for the sets
X,Y\subset[N]
such that
|X|=|Y|=k
, the claimed upper bound now follows from Lemma
5.13
(ii). This completes the proof.
∎

Next we derive bound on the second moment of
{\det}_{k}(z)
for
k\geqslant\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

In the setup of Lemma
6.1
, we have the following bounds, for all large
N
.

(i)

Fix
\gamma^{\prime}
with
1<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
. Then for any
k\in\mathds{N}
such that
\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}\leqslant k\leqslant N
we have

\sup_{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(s)}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)\right|^{2}\right]=O\left(N^{-(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\cdot(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}\right),\quad s=1,2.

(ii)

For
k_{0}=\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}
we have

\sup_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(s)}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k_{0}}(z)\right|^{2}\right]=O(1),\quad s=1,2.

(iii)

For any
k\in\mathds{N}
such that
\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}\leqslant k\leqslant N
we have

\sup_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)\right|^{2}\right]=O\left(N^{-(\gamma-1)\cdot(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}\right).

Remark 6.3
.

Lemma
6.2
(ii) shows that the supremum of the second moment of
\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)
, for
k=k_{0}
, is well controlled in the tubes
\mathcal{T}^{d}
that are of small but fixed width,
whereas Lemma
6.2
(i) shows that the same can be said for any
k>k_{0}
only in the tubes
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d}
that are of of vanishing width. In part (iii) we see that one has such a control for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}
but, as will be seen during the course of the proof, those bounds fail for
z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(1)}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

We start with the proof of part (i), beginning with
s=1
. Denote

(6.4)

\mathfrak{d}_{q}:=\binom{q(\widehat{m}_{2}+1)+\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\right)^{q}.

As

(6.5)

|\eta_{\widehat{m}_{1}+1}(z)|\geqslant 1-(\gamma^{\prime}-1)\frac{\log N}{N},\qquad\mbox{ for }z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(1)}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}},

by Lemma
5.13
(i) we have that

(6.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)|^{2}\leqslant 2\widehat{C}_{\ref {lem:comb-bound-tube-1}}^{2\widetilde{m}(k+N_{+})}\cdot N^{2(\gamma^{\prime}-1)G(k,z)}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}\geqslant 0}\mathfrak{d}_{q}\mathfrak{d}_{q^{\prime}}\cdot\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,q)\right].

We claim that, for any
q\geqslant 0
,

(6.7)

\left|\{X,Y\subset[N]:|X|=|Y|=k\text{ and }\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,q)=1\}\right|\leqslant(2\widehat{m}_{2})!\binom{q+2\widehat{m}_{2}}{2\widehat{m}_{2}}\cdot\binom{N}{k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}}^{2}.

Indeed, upon recalling (
5.39
), we note that for a given
q\geqslant 0
,

\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,q)=1\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\max\left\{\max_{j=k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}+1}^{k}(N-x_{j}),\max_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}}y_{j}\right\}\leqslant q.

Thus the number of choices of
\{x_{j}\}_{j=k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}+1}^{k}
and
\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}}
is at most

q^{2(\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+})}\leqslant q^{2\widehat{m}_{2}}\leqslant(2\widehat{m}_{2})!\binom{q+2\widehat{m}_{2}}{2\widehat{m}_{2}}.

Now choose the remaining elements of
X
and
Y
to obtain (
6.7
).

Next applying (
5.50
) and (
5.54
) we have

\sum_{q^{\prime}\geqslant 0}\mathfrak{d}_{q^{\prime}}\leqslant\left(\frac{4(\widehat{m}_{2}+1)}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+1)+1}.

On the other hand, (
5.50
), (
5.54
), and (
6.7
) yield that

\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{q\geqslant 0}\mathfrak{d}_{q}\cdot\mathfrak{I}(X,Y,q)\right]\leqslant(2\widehat{m}_{2})!\cdot\left(\frac{4(\widehat{m}_{2}+2)}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\widehat{m}_{2}(k+N_{+}+3)+1}\cdot N^{2(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}k^{2\widehat{m}_{2}}(k!)^{-2}.

Plugging in the last two bounds in (
6.6
) and using that
G(k,z)\leqslant k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}
, we deduce that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,Y\subset[N]\\
|X|=|Y|=k\end{subarray}}|\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X,Y,z)|^{2}\leqslant\overline{C}_{1}^{\widetilde{m}(k+N_{+})}\cdot N^{2\gamma^{\prime}(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}\cdot k^{2\widehat{m}_{2}}\cdot(k!)^{-2},

for some constant
\overline{C}_{1}<\infty
. Upon using (
6.3
) part(i) of the lemma follows for
s=1
.

Turning to
s=2
we observe that, as

G(k,\cdot)\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(2)}\supset\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}^{d,(2)}
for all large
N
, the upper bound (
6.6
) continues to hold in with
N^{2(\gamma^{\prime}-1)G(k,z)}
replaced by one. Thus, repeating the rest of the arguments we derive part (i) for
s=2
, as well as part (iii). The proof of part (ii) is exactly the same, where we again note that
G(k_{0},\cdot)\equiv 0
on
\mathcal{T}^{d,(1)}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}
and use that
k_{0}+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}=0
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

While applying Theorem
5.7
to derive the localization of the eigenvectors we will choose
1<\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
such that
(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})
is (fixed but) arbitrarily close to zero. For such choice of parameters, if
k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}
is
small
then Lemma
6.2
does not yield a sufficiently strong probability bound to carry out the covering argument. To overcome this caveat we control
high moments
of
{\det}_{k}(z)
for such choices of
k
, which when applied together with Markov’s inequality produce desired probability bounds.

Lemma 6.4
.

Consider the same setup as in Lemma
6.2
.
There exists a constant
\widehat{C}_{\ref {lem:high-mom-small-k}}<\infty
(depending on
p(\cdot)
and
\varepsilon_{0}
only) so that,
for all
h,k,K_{0}\in\mathds{N}
such that
\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}\leqslant k\leqslant K_{0}-N_{+}
, we have

(6.8)

\sup_{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(1)}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)\right|^{2h}\right]\leqslant C_{\ref {lem:high-mom-small-k}}N^{-2h(\gamma-\gamma^{\prime})\cdot(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}

and

(6.9)

\sup_{z\in\mathcal{T}^{d,(2)}_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)\right|^{2h}\right]\leqslant C_{\ref {lem:high-mom-small-k}}N^{-2h(\gamma-1)\cdot(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})},

where

C_{\ref {lem:high-mom-small-k}}=C_{\ref {lem:high-mom-small-k}}(h,K_{0},\widetilde{m},\widehat{m}_{2}):=\widehat{C}_{\ref {lem:high-mom-small-k}}^{\widetilde{m}K_{0}h}\cdot(K_{0}h)^{8K_{0}h}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{2K_{0}h}.

Proof.

We start with the proof of (
6.8
). Note that it suffices to show that for any
z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}^{d,(1)}_{\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}}
,

(6.10)

\mathfrak{T}_{k}(z):=N^{2\gamma h(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\widehat{\det}_{k}(z)\right|^{2h}\right]\leqslant\widehat{C}_{\ref {lem:high-mom-small-k}}^{\widetilde{m}K_{0}h}\cdot(K_{0}h)^{8K_{0}h}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{2K_{0}h}\cdot N^{2h\gamma^{\prime}(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}.

Turning to prove (
6.10
), using (
5.9
), (
5.32
), and (
5.35
)-(
5.36
) we find that

(6.11)

\mathfrak{T}_{k}(z)=\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{X}}}\sum_{\bm{Y}}\prod_{i=1}^{2h}(-1)^{\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{X^{(i)}})\operatorname{sgn}(\sigma_{Y^{(i)}})}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{h}\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X^{(i)},Y^{(i)},z)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{h}\det(Q[X^{(i)};Y^{(i)}])\\
\cdot\prod_{i=h+1}^{2h}\overline{\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}(X^{(i)},Y^{(i)},z)}\cdot\prod_{i=h+1}^{2h}\overline{\det(Q[X^{(i)};Y^{(i)}])}\Bigg],

where the sum is taken over

{\bm{X}}:=\{X^{(1)},X^{(2)},\ldots,X^{(2h)}\}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\bm{Y}}:=\{Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)},\ldots,Y^{(2h)}\},

and for
i\in[2h]
,

X^{(i)}:=\{x^{(i)}_{1}<x^{(i)}_{2}<\cdots<x^{(i)}_{k}\}\subset[N]\qquad\text{ and }\qquad Y^{(i)}:=\{y^{(i)}_{1}<y^{(i)}_{2}<\cdots<y^{(i)}_{k}\}\subset[N].

Associate to
{\bm{X}}
the partition
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{X}}}
determined by the equivalence relation

x^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}}\sim x^{(i^{\prime\prime})}_{j^{\prime\prime}}
iff
x^{(i^{\prime})}_{j^{\prime}}=x^{(i^{\prime\prime})}_{j^{\prime\prime}}
, for some
i^{\prime}\neq i^{\prime\prime}\in[2h]
and
j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}\in[k]
.
Similarly, associate to
{\bm{Y}}
the partition
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{Y}}}
.

As the entries of
Q
are independent and are of zero mean it is straightforward to observe that for any
({\bm{X}},{\bm{Y}})
such that either
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{X}}}
or
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{Y}}}
has an equivalence class of size one, the expectation of the summand on the
rhs
of (
6.11
) equals zero.
Hence, to compute a bound on the
rhs
of (
6.11
) we only need to consider partitions
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{X}}}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{Y}}}
such that each of their equivalence classes has size at least two. For brevity we term them
pair partitions
. Fix one such pair of partitions
(\mathfrak{P},\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}})
and a pair
({\bm{X}},{\bm{Y}})
such that
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{X}}}=\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{{\bm{Y}}}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}
.

For any
i\in[2h]
the determinant
\det(Q[X^{(i)};Y^{(i)}])
is a linear combination of
k!
terms each of which are products of
k
independent entries of the sub matrix
Q[X^{(i)};Y^{(i)}]
. Therefore denoting

\mathfrak{Q}:=\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\prod_{i=1}^{h}\det(Q[X^{(i)};Y^{(i)}])\cdot\overline{\prod_{i=h+1}^{2h}\det(Q[X^{(i)};Y^{(i)}])}\right|\right],

by the triangle inequality, we note that
\mathfrak{Q}
is a sum of at most
(k!)^{2h}
terms of the form

(6.12)

\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\prod_{w=1}^{b}Q_{u_{w},v_{w}}^{c_{w}}\cdot\overline{Q_{u_{w},v_{w}}^{c_{w}^{\prime}}}\right|\right],

for some
b\in\mathds{N}
and collections of positive integers
\{c_{w}\}_{w=1}^{b}
and
\{c^{\prime}_{w}\}_{w=1}^{b}
such that

\sum_{w=1}^{b}c_{w}=\sum_{w=1}^{b}c^{\prime}_{w}=kh,

where
Q_{u_{w},v_{w}}
denotes the
(u_{w},v_{w})
-th entry of
Q
and the collection
\{(u_{w},v_{w})\}_{w=1}^{b}
is pairwise disjoint. Thus
\{Q_{u_{w},v_{w}}\}_{w=1}^{b}
are jointly independent.
Upon using Hölder’s inequality and Assumption
1.1
(ii) it follows that (
6.12
) is bounded above by

\prod_{w=1}^{b}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[|Q_{u_{w},v_{w}}|^{2kh}\right]\right)^{\frac{c_{w}+c^{\prime}_{w}}{2kh}}\leqslant\mathfrak{C}_{2kh}\leqslant\mathfrak{C}_{2K_{0}h},

where in the last step we use that
k\leqslant K_{0}
and that
\mathfrak{C}_{w}
is increasing in
w
. This, in turn implies that
\mathfrak{Q}\leqslant K_{0}^{2K_{0}h}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{2K_{0}h}
. Plugging this bound in (
6.11
), and applying Lemma
5.13
(i), (
6.5
), and that
G(k,z)\leqslant k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}
, we now deduce that

(6.13)

\mathfrak{T}_{k}(z)\leqslant 2\widehat{C}_{\ref {lem:comb-bound-tube-1}}^{2h\widetilde{m}(k+N_{+})}\cdot N^{2h(\gamma^{\prime}-1)\cdot(k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2})}\cdot K_{0}^{2K_{0}h}\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{2K_{0}h}\cdot\\
\left\{\sum_{\mathfrak{P},\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}}\left[\sum_{{\bm{q}}_{\#}}\sum_{\bm{Y}:\mathfrak{P}_{\bm{Y}}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}}\sum_{\bm{X}:\mathfrak{P}_{\bm{X}}=\mathfrak{P}}\prod_{i=1}^{2h}\mathfrak{d}_{q^{(i)}}\cdot\mathfrak{I}(X^{(i)},Y^{(i)},q^{(i)})\right]\right\},

where
{\bm{q}}_{\#}:=(q^{(1)},q^{(2)},\ldots,q^{(2h)})
, and the outer sum is over all pair partitions
\mathfrak{P}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}
.
To evaluate the sum in (
6.13
) we will compute the sum in a specific order. To execute this step we need a few definitions.

We split the elements of
{\bm{X}}
and
{\bm{Y}}
into generating and non-generating elements as follows: For any
i\in[2h]
we term the collection of elements
\{x^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j=k+N_{+}-\widehat{m}_{2}+1}^{k}
and
\{y^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\widehat{m}_{2}-N_{+}}
